# What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle

Peilin Wang    Feng Shiyang    Hongfu Gao Affiliation: Shandong
University  National University of Singapore    Cencheng Zhao    Di Yuan
   Hui Chen Affiliation: Tsinghua University^(\*)Equal contribution.   
Guiguang Ding Affiliation: Tsinghua University^(\*)Equal contribution.

###### Abstract

Recursive computation repeatedly compresses or reuses intermediate
states, creating a simple tension: information that must remain useful
across longer recursive paths is also exposed to more opportunities for
loss before reaching the final prediction. Existing reconstruction or
local-prediction objectives provide tractable supervision, but do not
ensure that the retained information remains sufficient for subsequent
recursive computation. We identify local predictive sufficiency with
recursive predictive closure: controlling local predictive deficiencies
at individual interfaces controls the resulting discrepancy at the root.
We then turn this principle into a tractable training procedure.
Starting from a variational characterization, we derive finite
predictive tests and an empirical predictive deficiency that measures
predictive value retained across compression. Its predictive
sensitivities define margin-relaxed half-space constraints on parameter
updates, and we project the host optimizer’s proposed update onto their
intersection only when predictive preservation would otherwise be
violated. Across temporal graphs, language memory,
vision-language-action control, and recursive self-improvement, the
method matches or improves the corresponding host models under matched
compression budgets, with larger gains under heavier recursive or memory
demands, while better preserving predictive information across
successive transformations. Crucially, the same task-agnostic
predictive-preservation principle is instantiated across all four
settings through host-compatible interventions while keeping the
endpoint task, backbone, and evaluation protocol fixed. These results
establish predictive preservation at recursive interfaces as a general
training principle for recursive compression.

## 1 Introduction

Recursive computation is widely used across modern learning systems,
including graph and tree-structured propagation ([Goller and Küchler,
1996](#bib.bib15); [Scarselli et al., 2009](#bib.bib16); [Tai et al.,
2015](#bib.bib2); [Wang et al., 2025](#bib.bib6); [Feng et al.,
2025](#bib.bib7); [Guo et al., 2025](#bib.bib8)), recurrent context
compression, embodied memory, and recursive self-improvement ([Kim et
al., 2024](#bib.bib22); [Shi et al., 2026](#bib.bib23); [Kim et al.,
2026](#bib.bib33)). Despite their different implementations, these
systems share a common structure: information is repeatedly summarized
into an intermediate state, passed through a recursive interface, and
reused by subsequent computation. Earlier information therefore
undergoes more rounds of aggregation and compression, and long-range
information must pass through more recursive interfaces before affecting
the final prediction. Thus, the information that must remain useful over
longer paths is exposed to more opportunities for loss along the way.
Compression may attenuate information ([Alon and Yahav,
2021](#bib.bib36); [Topping et al., 2022](#bib.bib37); [Banerjee et al.,
2022](#bib.bib38)).

At each recursive interface, the model therefore faces a concrete
question: what should the intermediate state retain? Reconstruction
objectives encourage it to preserve information needed to recover its
input ([Socher et al., 2011](#bib.bib1)), while predictive-state
approaches represent state through predictions of future observations
([Littman et al., 2001](#bib.bib43); [Kulesza et al., 2015](#bib.bib14);
[Hefny et al., 2018](#bib.bib17)). Information-theoretic criteria
characterize predictive information more directly ([Tishby et al.,
1999](#bib.bib9); [Tishby and Zaslavsky, 2015](#bib.bib18); [Aguerri and
Zaidi, 2018](#bib.bib10); [Poole et al., 2019](#bib.bib13)), but are
difficult to estimate and optimize for high-dimensional intermediate
states. Recursive computation therefore calls for a tractable criterion
that specifies what each interface should preserve for subsequent
prediction.

Local Predictive Sufficiency. A natural approach is to ask every
intermediate state to preserve information about the final prediction.
Yet the states most exposed to repeated compression are also those for
which the final target is separated from the recursive interface by more
subsequent transformations, making this preservation signal increasingly
indirect ([Polyanskiy and Wu, 2017](#bib.bib21)). We instead assess
preservation where compression occurs. At each recursive interface, we
ask whether the compressed state retains the predictive information
available before compression for the adjacent predictive pair at that
interface. We call this property _local predictive sufficiency_. Under
the recursive model in
Sec. [2](#S2 "2 Preliminaries and Problem Formulation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),
recursive predictive closure connects these local conditions to
protection of the root target. We then derive an empirical predictive
deficiency that approximates the predictive value lost across
compression. In projection-based instantiations, the optimizer first
produces a task-driven proposal, and predictive feasibility projection
modifies that proposal only when it violates the margin-relaxed
predictive constraints. The broader framework changes how information is
retained at recursive interfaces while leaving the endpoint task and
evaluation protocol unchanged.

We evaluate the framework in four settings where recursive state
compression arises naturally: temporal graphs, language memory,
vision-language-action control, and recursive self-improvement. Across
these settings, preserving predictive structure at recursive interfaces
consistently improves downstream performance, with larger gains when
relevant information must survive longer recursive chains. On
DailyDialog, our method consistently improves over the corresponding
CCM-merge host across the tested memory depths. On LIBERO-Mem T3, it
increases full-task success from $`1.7\pm 2.9\%`$ to $`11.7\pm 2.9\%`$
across three independently trained seeds. Similar depth-dependent gains
appear in temporal graphs and across recursive self-improvement
iterations. Mechanism analyses further show slower attenuation of the
predictive statistic across successive recursive interfaces.

Our contributions are threefold. (1) Theory. We formulate local
predictive sufficiency and establish recursive predictive closure and
root predictive stability. (2) Learning. We derive an empirical
predictive deficiency and use its predictive sensitivities to constrain
task proposals that would degrade predictive structure across recursive
interfaces. (3) Evaluation. We validate the principle in four recursive
settings and show that its benefits become more pronounced as
information must survive longer recursive chains. Together, these
contributions provide a common view of what recursive intermediate
states should preserve and a practical mechanism for protecting it
during learning.

## 2 Preliminaries and Problem Formulation

### 2.1 Recursive Compression Setup

Recursive setting. We unroll recursive computation over time into a
finite rooted tree with node set $`V`$ and root $`\rho`$, where each
$`v\in V\setminus\{\rho\}`$ denotes a recursive interface and $`\rho`$
denotes the root target site. We write $`\operatorname{pa}(v)`$ for the
next interface receiving information from $`v`$. At each non-root
interface $`v`$, $`U_{v}`$ denotes the state assembled from the
information available in the corresponding subtree immediately before
compression. The variable $`C_{v}`$ denotes the observed interface
information that is retained directly across this update without passing
through the compression map, and $`C:=(C_{v})_{v\in V}`$ collects these
retained contexts. A measurable compression map $`\Gamma`$ produces the
width-$`r`$ representation $`Z_{v}^{\Gamma}:=\Gamma(U_{v})`$, which is
then passed to the remaining recursive computation.

Predictive task. Each recursive interface $`v`$ is associated with an
observed predictive event $`Y_{v}\in\mathcal{Y}`$,¹¹ 1 Examples include
interaction outcomes, subsequent responses, and recorded actions. The
same observed event may be associated with multiple recursive
interfaces, while intermediate transformations without an associated
event are included in the recursive transition between adjacent
recursive interfaces. with $`Y_{\rho}`$ denoting the root target. Our
goal is to learn $`\Gamma`$ such that repeated compression preserves the
predictive information required for $`Y_{\rho}`$. The Information
Bottleneck frames compression relative to a relevance variable ([Tishby
et al., 1999](#bib.bib9); [Tishby and Zaslavsky, 2015](#bib.bib18)), and
future outcomes provide a natural choice for predictive representations
([Still, 2014](#bib.bib19); [Marzen and Crutchfield, 2016](#bib.bib20)).
We formalize this requirement through predictive sufficiency.

###### Definition 2.1 (Predictive Sufficiency).

Following statistical sufficiency ([Cover and Thomas,
2006](#bib.bib24)), $`Z_{v}^{\Gamma}`$ is _predictively sufficient_ for
target $`Y`$ given $`C`$ if

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\mathcal{L}(Y\mid U_{v},C)=\mathcal{L}(Y\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C)\qquad\text{a.s.},
``` |  |

or equivalently,

|     |                                    |     |
|-----|------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       I(Y;U_{v}\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C)=0.
       ```                                 |     |

### 2.2 Predictive Preservation under Recursive Structure

For each $`v\in V`$, let $`Y_{v}`$ denote the predictive event
associated with that recursive stage, with $`Y_{\rho}`$ denoting the
root target. The root target $`Y_{\rho}`$ is the global relevance
variable, since it is ultimately the prediction that compression should
preserve. For a deeper state, however, its relation to $`Y_{\rho}`$ is
mediated by a longer sequence of recursive transformations. By the
data-processing principle, mutual information cannot increase along such
a chain and may contract under repeated transformations ([Polyanskiy and
Wu, 2017](#bib.bib21)). Hence, as recursive depth grows, the root target
can become an increasingly indirect guide for deciding what information
an intermediate state should retain.

Recursive computation provides a way to exploit nearby predictive
events. Once unrolled as a rooted tree, information from a subtree
reaches the rest of the computation through successive parent–child
interfaces, connecting nearby predictive events along the same rootward
path. Proximity alone does not determine relevance to the root target.
Conditional on $`C`$, the tree structure induces a local separation:
subtrees that branch apart interact only through their shared ancestral
path ([Lauritzen, 1996](#bib.bib25)). At each recursive interface,
incoming child representations are aggregated into $`U_{v}`$ before
recursive propagation. We require the pre-compression interface state to
screen the adjacent predictive events from those incoming child states
once $`(U_{v},C)`$ is given.
Appendix [F.1](#A6.SS1 "F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
states this condition as (M3), together with the predictive-tree
condition (M1) and recursive host construction (M2). When the complete
interface record $`U_{v}`$ contains the incoming child tuple, (M3) holds
automatically. This naturally motivates using adjacent predictive events
along each rootward path. For every non-root recursive interface $`v`$,
define the adjacent predictive pair
$`S_{v}:=(Y_{v},Y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)})`$. Applying
Definition [2.1](#S2.Thmdefinition1 "Definition 2.1 (Predictive Sufficiency). ‣ 2.1 Recursive Compression Setup ‣ 2 Preliminaries and Problem Formulation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
to $`S_{v}`$ gives local predictive sufficiency. Consecutive pairs share
an intermediate predictive event, allowing predictive sufficiency to
compose recursively toward the root.

###### Proposition 1 (Recursive Predictive Closure).

Suppose the recursive computation satisfies (M1)–(M3) of
Appendix [F.1](#A6.SS1 "F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
Let $`U:=(U_{v})_{v\neq\rho}`$ and
$`Z^{\Gamma}:=(Z_{v}^{\Gamma})_{v\neq\rho}`$. Then

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\left[I(S_{v};U_{v}\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C)=0,\quad\forall\,v\in V\setminus\{\rho\}\right]\Longrightarrow I(Y_{\rho};U\mid Z^{\Gamma},C)=0.
``` |  |

Proposition [1](#Thmprop1 "Proposition 1 (Recursive Predictive Closure). ‣ 2.2 Predictive Preservation under Recursive Structure ‣ 2 Preliminaries and Problem Formulation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
shows that root-target sufficiency follows from local predictive
sufficiency at the recursive interfaces. If $`Z_{v}^{\Gamma}`$ preserves
the information in $`U_{v}`$ required for $`S_{v}`$ at every recursive
interface, the shared predictive events allow these conditions to
compose toward $`Y_{\rho}`$. The proof is given in
Appendix [F.1](#A6.SS1 "F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
This motivates the local predictive deficiency
$`I(S_{v};U_{v}\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C)`$ under a prescribed compression
width. Driving these local predictive deficiencies to zero recovers the
root-level guarantee. Since the criterion depends on unknown conditional
laws, direct estimation is difficult for finite, high-dimensional
representations.
Section [3](#S3 "3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
develops a tractable empirical realization.

## 3 Learning for Predictive Preservation

### 3.1 Predictive Deficiency Estimation

Local predictive sufficiency specifies what compression should preserve:
replacing the pre-compression state $`U_{v}`$ with $`Z_{v}^{\Gamma}`$
should retain the information needed to predict the adjacent predictive
pair $`S_{v}`$. The exact variational criterion
$`\inf_{q_{v}}\mathcal{V}_{v}(\Gamma,q_{v})`$ captures this requirement,
but depends on an unknown conditional law and is unavailable during
training. We make it tractable by restricting prediction to finite
tests, profiling the predictive readout, and estimating the resulting
population quantity from finite samples.
Figure [1](#S3.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 3.1 Predictive Deficiency Estimation ‣ 3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
summarizes this construction; formal derivations and approximation
bounds are given in
Appendices [F.2](#A6.SS2 "F.2 Local Variational Characterization ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
and [F.3](#A6.SS3 "F.3 From Local Predictive Deficiency to Constrained Updates ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

![Refer to caption](2610.04303v1/tex3-1_reasoning_chain.png)

Figure 1: From the exact predictive criterion to empirical predictive
deficiency. The construction uses finite tests, covariance-balanced
readouts, and estimates the population quantity.

Restricting to finite predictive tests. Because the conditional law of
$`S_{v}`$ is unavailable, we replace the full-law comparison by the
fixed test representation
$`P_{v}:=T_{S}\!\left(\chi(C_{v})\otimes\phi(Y_{v},Y_{\mathrm{pa}(v)})\right)`$,
where $`\chi`$ and $`\phi`$ are fixed feature maps and $`T_{S}`$ is a
bounded-width sketch. For a candidate representation $`X_{v}`$, let
$`\mathcal{R}_{v}^{\star}(P_{v}\mid X_{v},C_{v}):=\inf_{g}\mathbb{E}[\|P_{v}-g(X_{v},C_{v})\|_{2}^{2}]`$
denote its optimal predictive risk. The finite-test predictive risk gap
$`\Delta_{v}^{\mathrm{test}}(\Gamma):=\mathcal{R}_{v}^{\star}(P_{v}\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C_{v})-\mathcal{R}_{v}^{\star}(P_{v}\mid U_{v},C_{v})`$
measures the predictive loss induced by compression on this fixed test
family. The localization from the retained global context $`C`$ to the
visible $`C_{v}`$ is characterized in
Appendix [F.3](#A6.SS3 "F.3 From Local Predictive Deficiency to Constrained Updates ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

Profiling the predictive readout. The finite-test risk still optimizes
over unrestricted measurable predictors. We therefore restrict the
readout to ridge regression and profile out its parameters analytically.
Full-covariance balancing then yields the profiled balanced predictive
value $`J_{\mathrm{bal},v}`$, giving
$`\Delta_{v}^{\mathrm{bal}}(\Gamma):=J_{\mathrm{bal},v}(U_{v}\mid C_{v})-J_{\mathrm{bal},v}(Z_{v}^{\Gamma}\mid C_{v})`$.
Here $`\mathcal{R}_{v}^{\star}`$ is a risk to be minimized, whereas
$`J_{\mathrm{bal},v}`$ is a predictive value to be maximized, hence the
reversed difference.

Estimating predictive deficiency from finite samples. We replace the
remaining population covariance operators by regularized empirical
estimates over an optimization window $`\mathcal{B}`$, so that the
predictive geometry is estimated from a shared set of recursive
realizations, which then yields
$`\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\Gamma):=\widehat{J}_{\mathrm{bal},\mathcal{B}}(U)-\widehat{J}_{\mathrm{bal},\mathcal{B}}(Z^{\Gamma})`$.
The pre-compression term is treated as a stop-gradient reference, so the
first-order sensitivities used for optimization are taken through
$`Z^{\Gamma}`$ while the reference predictive value remains fixed within
the window. Full-covariance balancing is used in the main method, while
the diagonal form is evaluated only as a covariance approximation in the
ablation study.
Section [3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Margin-Relaxed Predictive Constraints ‣ 3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
uses these predictive sensitivities to constrain task proposals.

### 3.2 Margin-Relaxed Predictive Constraints

Task optimization may improve the root-target objective while increasing
empirical predictive deficiency at intermediate recursive interfaces. We
therefore constrain task proposals using these predictive sensitivities,
with a margin for task adaptation. For a realized compressed state
$`z_{i}^{\Gamma}`$, let
$`a_{i}:=\nabla_{z_{i}^{\Gamma}}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\Gamma)`$
denote its predictive sensitivity in representation space. It describes
how the empirical predictive deficiency changes under a perturbation of
that compressed state. Pulling this sensitivity back through the
compressor Jacobian gives
$`q_{i}:=(\nabla_{\Gamma}z_{i}^{\Gamma})^{\top}a_{i}`$ in the trainable
parameter space. For a small update $`d`$, $`q_{i}^{\top}d`$ measures
first-order contribution to the change in empirical predictive
deficiency. Thus, $`a_{i}`$ identifies the predictive sensitivity of the
compressed representation, while $`q_{i}`$ is the corresponding
predictive sensitivity in the trainable parameter space. Because all
sensitivities are differentiated from the same window-level statistic
$`\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathcal{B}}`$, they reflect a shared empirical
surrogate, not distinct population $`\delta_{v}`$ values.

We aggregate these local directions at the granularity of the predictive
constraints within the current optimization window. For group
$`\mathcal{G}_{g}`$, let
$`\bar{q}_{g}:=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}_{g}}q_{i}`$ and
$`h_{g}:=\bar{q}_{g}/\|\bar{q}_{g}\|_{2}`$ when $`\bar{q}_{g}\neq 0`$;
zero groups impose no constraint. In temporal graphs, each group
contains realizations from one computation tree and one interface type,
so each constraint captures their joint first-order effect without
introducing additional depth-dependent weighting. Stacking
$`h_{g}^{\top}`$ gives $`H_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times p}`$. For the task
proposal $`t_{t}`$, define $`b_{t}:=\kappa\|t_{t}\|_{2}\mathbf{1}_{m}`$
and $`\mathcal{F}_{t}:=\{d:H_{t}d\leq b_{t}\}`$. Each row of $`H_{t}`$
limits motion along one predictive sensitivity, and their intersection
defines the predictive-feasible region. The tolerance $`\kappa\geq 0`$
controls the allowed first-order increase in predictive deficiency.
Figure  shows how the margin-relaxed predictive constraints restrict the
task proposal to yield a predictive-feasible update.

###### Proposition 2 (Dual Characterization of Predictive Feasibility Projection).

The predictive feasibility projection of the task proposal onto
$`\mathcal{F}_{t}`$ has a unique constrained update $`d_{t}^{\star}`$.
There exists a nonnegative dual optimal multiplier, and every dual
optimum yields the same $`d_{t}^{\star}`$. Moreover, the predictive
correction lies in the span of the predictive constraint directions.
Specifically,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\alpha_{t}^{\star}\in\underset{\alpha\geq 0}{\operatorname{argmax}}\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{\top}(H_{t}H_{t}^{\top})\alpha+\alpha^{\top}(H_{t}t_{t}-b_{t})\right\},\qquad d_{t}^{\star}=t_{t}-H_{t}^{\top}\alpha_{t}^{\star}.
``` |  |

Proposition [2](#Thmprop2 "Proposition 2 (Dual Characterization of Predictive Feasibility Projection). ‣ 3.2 Margin-Relaxed Predictive Constraints ‣ 3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
gives a tractable characterization of the constrained update. The
correction is restricted to the span of the predictive constraint
directions, while the dual optimization is carried out in the
$`m`$-dimensional constraint space rather than the full
$`p`$-dimensional parameter space. The primal–dual derivation,
feasibility properties, and computational complexity are given in
Appendix [F.3](#A6.SS3 "F.3 From Local Predictive Deficiency to Constrained Updates ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle");
solver and feasibility settings are reported in
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
The dual characterization permits any nonnegative $`b_{t}`$;
$`b_{t}=\kappa\|t_{t}\|_{2}\mathbf{1}_{m}`$ is our full-covariance
choice.

### 3.3 Predictive Feasibility Optimization

 

\thealgorithm Predictive Feasibility Optimization

 

Require:

Window $`\mathcal{B}`$, compressor $`\Gamma_{t}`$, optimizer
$`\mathcal{O}`$ with state $`s_{t}`$, tolerance $`\kappa`$

Ensure: Updated compressor $`\Gamma_{t+1}`$

1.  1.
    Run the host forward pass and cache $`(P,U,Z^{\Gamma})`$
2.  2.
    Construct the grouped predictive constraints $`(H_{t},b_{t})`$ from
    the window statistics and tolerance $`\kappa`$
3.  3.
    Obtain the task proposal
    $`t_{t}\leftarrow\Delta_{\mathcal{O}}(\Gamma_{t},s_{t},\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{task}})`$
4.  4.
    Solve for $`d_{t}^{\star}`$ using
    Proposition [2](#Thmprop2 "Proposition 2 (Dual Characterization of Predictive Feasibility Projection). ‣ 3.2 Margin-Relaxed Predictive Constraints ‣ 3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
5.  5.
    Advance $`s_{t}`$ once and set
    $`\Gamma_{t+1}\leftarrow\Gamma_{t}+d_{t}^{\star}`$

 

Algorithm [2](#S3.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 3.3 Predictive Feasibility Optimization ‣ 3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
summarizes the training procedure. Within each optimization window, the
host forward pass provides the predictive statistics, from which the
interface sensitivities are pooled into the grouped constraint matrix
$`H_{t}`$ defined in
Section [3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Margin-Relaxed Predictive Constraints ‣ 3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
The host optimizer produces the task proposal $`t_{t}`$ on the trainable
compressor parameters.
Proposition [2](#Thmprop2 "Proposition 2 (Dual Characterization of Predictive Feasibility Projection). ‣ 3.2 Margin-Relaxed Predictive Constraints ‣ 3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
then maps $`(H_{t},t_{t})`$ to the constrained update $`d_{t}^{\star}`$,
which is written back to $`\Gamma`$. The host forward computation and
task objective remain unchanged.

Optimization procedure. The optimizer preview constructs the task
proposal $`t_{t}`$ without advancing its state. Predictive feasibility
projection then returns the closest constrained update in the
predictive-feasible region defined by $`(H_{t},b_{t})`$. The optimizer
state is advanced once per window, and the resulting constrained update
$`d_{t}^{\star}`$ is written back to the compressor parameters.

![Refer to caption](2610.04303v1/tex_3-2_method.png)

Figure 3: Predictive feasibility optimization for recursive compression.
Predictive sensitivities constrain the task proposal before parameter
write-back.

### 3.4 Properties and Predictive Guarantees

Local predictive deficiencies admit a quantitative global consequence.
We now relate accumulated local predictive deficiencies to the
predictive law of the root target.

###### Proposition 3 (Root Predictive Stability).

Under (M1)–(M3) of
Appendix [F.1](#A6.SS1 "F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),
with $`C`$ the full retained context and $`Z_{v}^{\Gamma}=U_{v}`$ at any
uncompressed interface,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}\!\left[\operatorname{TV}\!\left(\mathcal{L}(Y_{\rho}\mid U,C),\mathcal{L}(Y_{\rho}\mid Z^{\Gamma},C)\right)\right]\leq\left\{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{v\neq\rho}I\!\left(S_{v};U_{v}\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C\right)\right\}^{1/2}.
``` |  |

Proposition [3](#Thmprop3 "Proposition 3 (Root Predictive Stability). ‣ 3.4 Properties and Predictive Guarantees ‣ 3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
provides a quantitative local-to-global guarantee. It shows that the
change in the root predictive law is controlled by the local predictive
deficiencies at the individual recursive interfaces, so small local
predictive deficiencies imply a small global predictive discrepancy.
When every local predictive deficiency vanishes, the compressed
computation preserves the root target exactly, recovering the conclusion
of
Proposition [1](#Thmprop1 "Proposition 1 (Recursive Predictive Closure). ‣ 2.2 Predictive Preservation under Recursive Structure ‣ 2 Preliminaries and Problem Formulation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
This guarantee concerns the population-level local predictive
deficiencies and applies when its stated local predictive deficiencies
are controlled. The local calibration in
Corollary [F.1](#A6.Thmcorollary1 "Corollary F.1 (Controlled surrogate for the local predictive deficiency). ‣ Finite-sample moments. ‣ F.3.4 Empirical Predictive Deficiency ‣ F.3 From Local Predictive Deficiency to Constrained Updates ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
requires additional test-coverage, readout, context-localization, and
independent-sample conditions; the projection bounds only a first-order
change in the pooled training statistic.
Appendix [F.4](#A6.SS4 "F.4 Finite-Horizon Control along Predictive Feasibility Optimization ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
further shows that the projected updates bound the finite-horizon change
of the pooled empirical predictive deficiency, up to the relaxation
margin and residual higher-order terms. Combined with the local
calibration above, this connects the quantity controlled during training
to the population deficiencies in
Proposition [3](#Thmprop3 "Proposition 3 (Root Predictive Stability). ‣ 3.4 Properties and Predictive Guarantees ‣ 3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle");
details are given in the appendix. The complete proof is given in
Appendix [F.5](#A6.SS5 "F.5 Proof of Root Predictive Stability ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

Local supervision beyond the root target. Root-target supervision
identifies intermediate states through their root-target posterior.
Appendix [F.6](#A6.SS6 "F.6 Root-Target Supervision Does Not Imply Local Predictive Sufficiency ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
shows that this quotient may merge states with distinct recursive
posteriors: root-target sufficiency can hold while
$`I(S_{v};U_{v}\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C)`$ remains positive. Hence
root-target sufficiency alone does not imply local predictive
sufficiency. Our update constrains the grouped first-order change of the
predictive surrogate. At zero margin, each grouped constraint has a
non-positive linearized contribution, while a feasible task proposal is
unchanged.
Appendix [F.3.5](#A6.SS3.SSS5 "F.3.5 Interface Constraints and Projection ‣ F.3 From Local Predictive Deficiency to Constrained Updates ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
proves this guarantee,
Appendix [F.3](#A6.SS3 "F.3 From Local Predictive Deficiency to Constrained Updates ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
quantifies the approximation to the local predictive criterion, and
Appendix [F.5](#A6.SS5 "F.5 Proof of Root Predictive Stability ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
relates the local predictive deficiencies to root predictive stability.

## 4 Experiments

##### Datasets and tasks.

We evaluate our method across four recursive settings. For temporal
graph learning, we use Wikipedia ([Kumar et al., 2019](#bib.bib26)) for
node classification and UCI ([Panzarasa et al., 2009](#bib.bib27)) for
link prediction, with propagation depths controlling recursive
neighborhood expansion. For language memory, we use DailyDialog ([Li et
al., 2017](#bib.bib29)) for response prediction across increasing
conversation histories and Multi-Session Chat (MSC) ([Xu et al.,
2022](#bib.bib35)) for long-term response prediction across increasing
session histories. For vision-language-action control, we use
LIBERO-Mem ([Chung et al., 2026](#bib.bib32)) to evaluate memory during
multi-stage manipulation under the T3 setting. For each training run,
checkpoint selection uses an internal 20-demonstration development split
from the released refined training data; the selected checkpoint is then
frozen and evaluated on a separate set of 20 official held-out
evaluation initializations. For SRI, we use a six-task cohort from
CO-Bench ([Sun et al., 2026](#bib.bib34)) as a mechanism-level
evaluation of predictive preservation, where optimization candidates are
recursively generated and refined from earlier candidates and execution
feedback, and retained history conditions subsequent strategy updates.
Detailed dataset statistics and matched evaluation protocols across all
four domains are provided in
Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Experimental Protocols and Evaluation Details ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

##### Models and baselines.

Across all four domains, we instantiate our method on a representative
recursive model: TGN ([Rossi et al., 2020](#bib.bib4)) for temporal
graphs, CCM-merge ([Kim et al., 2024](#bib.bib22)) for language memory,
MemoryVLA ([Shi et al., 2026](#bib.bib23)) for vision-language-action
control, and Meta^(n) ([Kim et al., 2026](#bib.bib33)) for SRI. For
temporal graphs, we compare against TGN ([Rossi et al.,
2020](#bib.bib4)), TGAT ([Xu et al., 2020](#bib.bib3)), TCL ([Wang et
al., 2021](#bib.bib30)), CTAN ([Gravina et al., 2024](#bib.bib31)), and
DyGFormer ([Yu et al., 2023](#bib.bib5)). For language memory, we
compare directly against the corresponding CCM-merge host under the same
backbone, memory depth, and evaluation protocol. For
vision-language-action control, we compare against MemoryVLA ([Shi et
al., 2026](#bib.bib23)) and report prior-work results for $`\pi_{0}`$,
SlotVLA, and E-SlotSSM on the same LIBERO-Mem T3 benchmark as contextual
references. For SRI, we compare against Meta^(n) ([Kim et al.,
2026](#bib.bib33)) with the same backbone, task cohort, recursive search
procedure, and optimization budget. Ours changes the historical-trace
retention rule used for subsequent recursive updates. Unless otherwise
specified, our method modifies only the compression, memory, or
recursive-context retention mechanism of the corresponding host while
keeping the underlying backbone and evaluation protocol fixed.

Table 1: Main results across recursive systems. Results span temporal
graphs, language memory, VLA control, and SRI; best results appear bold.

##### Metrics.

We use task-specific metrics across the four domains. For temporal
graphs, we report average precision (AP) and ROC-AUC. For language
memory, we report dialogue-equal perplexity (PPL) on DailyDialog and
session-opening PPL on MSC. For vision-language-action control, we
report task success rate (SR) and sequential-goal completion (SGC) under
T3. For SRI, we report Dev Gain from one paired recursive-search run
and, for each task modified during development-side recursive
refinement, the mean held-out score over three matched evaluations of
each frozen strategy. The complete six-task task-wise results and
cohort-level average from one fixed held-out evaluation are reported in
Appendix [D.4](#A4.SS4 "D.4 Recursive Self-Improvement Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
Together, these metrics measure task-level predictive or control quality
under each recursive setting and across different forms of recursion.

##### Main Findings.

As summarized in
Table [1](#S4.T1 "Table 1 ‣ Models and baselines. ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),
our method consistently improves the corresponding host across four
substantially different recursive systems by modifying only how
intermediate information is retained. A particularly informative pattern
emerges as recursive dependence becomes more demanding. In language
memory, the improvement over the compressed host is largest at longer
histories, where information must survive repeated compression. In
temporal graphs, although our TGN-based method trails the strong
DyGFormer baseline at shallower propagation on UCI, this gap closes as
propagation deepens, reaching comparable AP and higher AUC at the
deepest setting. The VLA and SRI results extend this observation beyond
explicit depth axes: the same preservation mechanism remains effective
in long-horizon closed-loop execution and recursive strategy refinement,
where retained information is reused in later computation. Complete
depth- and history-resolved results are provided in
Appendices [D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Temporal Graph Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
and [D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Language Memory Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),
with additional long-horizon VLA and recursive-strategy analyses in
Appendices [D.3](#A4.SS3 "D.3 Vision–Language–Action Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
and [D.4](#A4.SS4 "D.4 Recursive Self-Improvement Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

(1) Are the end-task gains consistent with the predictive-preservation
mechanism? End-task improvements alone do not identify why the method
works. We therefore use
Fig. [4](#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Main Findings. ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
as a mechanism-consistency analysis. In temporal graphs,
Fig. [4](#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Main Findings. ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")(a)
shows that our method retains stronger finite-test predictive value
$`J`$ across recursive interfaces than TGN and task-only training,
including predictive information originating from deeper sources. In
SRI,
Fig. [4](#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Main Findings. ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")(b)
shows that information retained from earlier executions remains more
useful when reused during subsequent strategy refinement. The same
association also appears during language-memory training:
Appendix [D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Language Memory Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),
Fig. [6](#A4.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ Training-time predictive diagnostics. ‣ D.2 Language Memory Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),
shows that the predictive-preservation signal $`\Delta J`$ and
validation PPL improvement evolve broadly in the same direction over the
training trajectory. These diagnostics do not directly estimate the
population deficiency $`\delta_{v}`$; together, they provide
mechanism-level evidence linking task gains to stronger predictive
preservation and downstream reuse.

(a) Predictive information preservation

(b) Future utility of retained information

Figure 4: Mechanism analysis. (a) The finite-test predictive value $`J`$
tracks predictive distinctions retained across recursive interfaces. (b)
Future gain retained measures whether retained information remains
useful when reused in later recursive updates. Together, the two
diagnostics test whether end-task improvements are accompanied by
stronger predictive preservation and downstream reuse.

(2) Does the optimization actually enforce the intended preservation
constraint? The mechanism diagnostics above characterize the learned
representations, but do not by themselves show whether the constraint is
active during optimization.
Appendix [D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Temporal Graph Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),
Fig. [5](#A4.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ D.1 Temporal Graph Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),
directly examines this behavior: unconstrained task proposals continue
to violate a nonzero fraction of the grouped predictive constraints
throughout training, although the violation density decreases as
optimization progresses. Thus, the projection is not vacuous; task
optimization continues to propose directions that can interfere with
predictive preservation. Moreover,
Table [9](#A4.T9 "Table 9 ‣ D.1 Temporal Graph Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
shows that the constrained first-order quantity closely tracks the
actual change in the corresponding window-level predictive statistic,
validating the local bridge used by the projection.
Table [2](#S4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Main Findings. ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
further separates this constrained update from task-only training, an
additive predictive-gradient update, and a single global predictive
constraint, while the complete controlled ablations are reported in
Appendix [E.7](#A5.SS7 "E.7 Ablation Study ‣ Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
Their degradation relative to the full method supports the role of
interface-wise constrained optimization beyond merely adding predictive
supervision.

|                                     |                 |                  |
|-------------------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| Variant                             | AP $`\uparrow`$ | AUC $`\uparrow`$ |
| Task-Only Training                  | 92.62           | 92.13            |
| Direct Future Prediction            | 92.76           | 92.24            |
| Single-Event Predictive Criterion   | 92.41           | 91.93            |
| Additive Predictive-Gradient Update | 92.64           | 92.18            |
| Global Predictive Constraint        | 90.32           | 89.88            |
| Our Method                          | 94.52           | 94.25            |

Table 2: Single-seed diagnostic ablation on UCI at depth 3. We isolate
the roles of the predictive target, the adjacent predictive pair, and
the way predictive supervision constrains updates.

Ablation studies.
Table [2](#S4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Main Findings. ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
isolates the main design choices under the same UCI depth-3 setting.
Task-Only Training controls for the shared host, compressor, and
trainable parameterization, testing whether additional learnability
alone explains the improvement. Direct Future Prediction asks whether a
conventional future-prediction objective can replace the proposed
predictive criterion, while the Single-Event Predictive Criterion
directly tests the adjacent predictive pair $`S_{v}`$ underlying our
theoretical formulation. Additive Predictive-Gradient Update uses the
same predictive supervision as an auxiliary gradient, separating the
contribution of predictive supervision from its constrained
parameter-space realization. Global Predictive Constraint further tests
whether an aggregated constraint can replace the interface-wise
structure used by our method. Across these controls, the observed
performance degradations are substantial, supporting the respective
roles of the predictive criterion, adjacent predictive pair, and
constrained update. Additional ablations on statistical realization and
supervision alignment are reported in
Appendix [E.7](#A5.SS7 "E.7 Ablation Study ‣ Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

Robustness and efficiency. We further examine the two choices that most
directly control the constraint. The feasibility tolerance $`\kappa`$
exhibits a clear interior optimum: overly small values restrict the task
proposal too strongly, whereas larger values weaken predictive
preservation. Increasing the eligible-interface fraction improves test
AP and then approaches saturation, indicating that the pooled statistic
benefits from broader interface coverage once sufficient support is
available. We therefore select $`\kappa`$ by a small validation sweep
and retain broad eligible-interface coverage. The method acts only
during training; its additional computation consists of pooled minibatch
statistics, predictive sensitivities, and the predictive feasibility
projection, while test-time computation remains that of the host. The
complete sensitivity curves are reported in
Appendix [E.8](#A5.SS8 "E.8 Method Hyperparameters and Sensitivity ‣ Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

## 5 Conclusion

We study how to learn compact states for recursive computation without
losing information required by prediction. Our approach builds on local
predictive deficiency and recursive predictive closure, and uses
predictive feasibility projection to constrain the task proposal. Across
temporal graphs, language memory, vision-language-action control, and
recursive self-improvement, the method improves host models under
matched compression budgets. Mechanistic analyses show preservation of
predictive information from deeper sources, while ablations confirm the
roles of alignment, conditioning, and constrained optimization. These
results support predictive preservation at recursive interfaces as a
principle for compact states across domains.

Limitations. Our formulation relies on statistical support for
estimating local predictive constraints, which may become less stable in
data-sparse regimes. The feasibility tolerance $`\kappa`$ also requires
validation-based calibration and may vary across tasks and domains.
Developing more data-efficient estimators and adaptive calibration
strategies, including transfer across related tasks, remains a direction
for future work. The VLA evidence is restricted to the held-out
LIBERO-Mem T3 evaluation initializations and should therefore be
interpreted as held-out closed-loop performance under this benchmark
setting rather than as broad out-of-distribution generalization.

## Appendix A AI Use Statement

Generative AI tools were used to assist with several supporting aspects
of this work. They were used to polish and improve the clarity of the
manuscript, while the technical content and final wording were reviewed
and controlled by the authors. They were also used for literature and
repository retrieval and discovery; all cited papers, repositories, and
links were subsequently verified by the authors against the original
sources.

Generative AI was additionally used to draft portions of standard
descriptive text, such as preliminary summaries of experimental settings
or results, which were subsequently revised by the authors based on the
underlying experiments and technical details. For mathematical claims,
the claims and main proof ideas were developed by the authors, while
generative AI was used to assist with intermediate derivations,
formalization, and proof checking during iterative development. All
final mathematical statements and proofs were reviewed and verified by
the authors.

For software development, the authors designed the code structure,
implementation logic, and experimental pipeline, while generative AI was
used to assist with code implementation. All AI-assisted code was
subsequently reviewed, checked, and validated by the authors.

The authors take full responsibility for the final content of the paper.

## Appendix B Related Work

Recursive state compression appears across a broad range of modern
learning systems. Temporal graph models repeatedly aggregate historical
interactions into evolving node representations, as in TGN and TGAT
\[[Rossi et al., 2020](#bib.bib4), [Xu et al., 2020](#bib.bib3)\];
recurrent language-memory methods compress accumulated context into
states that are reused across subsequent segments or interactions, as in
CCM and AutoCompressor \[[Kim et al., 2024](#bib.bib22), [Chevalier et
al., 2023](#bib.bib39)\]; embodied models such as MemoryVLA maintain
compact perceptual–cognitive memories across multi-stage control \[[Shi
et al., 2026](#bib.bib23)\]; and iterative self-improvement or
program-search systems reuse selected candidates, execution feedback, or
reflections across successive rounds, as in Reflexion, FunSearch, and
ReEvo \[[Shinn et al., 2023](#bib.bib40), [Romera-Paredes et al.,
2024](#bib.bib41), [Ye et al., 2024](#bib.bib42)\]. Despite their
architectural differences, these systems share a common structure:
intermediate states are repeatedly aggregated, compressed, and reused.
This makes the central question not only how to compress a state, but
what information must survive compression so that subsequent computation
remains predictive.

Existing representation-learning methods impose different criteria for
deciding whether a compressed state is adequate. A classical approach
uses reconstruction. Recursive autoencoders, for example, compose child
representations into a parent state and train the parent representation
to reconstruct its children \[[Socher et al., 2011](#bib.bib1)\]. More
generally, autoencoding and masked-reconstruction objectives encourage
representations to retain enough information to recover designated
inputs or local content. A second family instead uses local or future
prediction. Contrastive Predictive Coding trains a context
representation to predict future latent representations \[[van den Oord
et al., 2018](#bib.bib50)\], while joint-embedding predictive approaches
such as I-JEPA predict representations of masked target regions from
visible context without reconstructing raw inputs \[[Assran et al.,
2023](#bib.bib51)\]. Such objectives place greater emphasis on
predictive structure than direct reconstruction. However, both
reconstruction and future-prediction objectives require a target to be
specified in advance. In a recursively composed system, accurately
reconstructing an immediate input, predicting an immediate parent, or
predicting a particular future latent does not by itself imply that the
target preserves all distinctions needed after subsequent aggregation
and compression.

Information-theoretic and predictive-state formulations provide a more
general perspective on what a representation should preserve. The
Information Bottleneck (IB) formalizes compression through retained
information about a designated relevance variable \[[Tishby et al.,
1999](#bib.bib9)\]; distributed and multivariate variants extend this
principle to multiple observations and interacting variables \[[Aguerri
and Zaidi, 2018](#bib.bib10), [Abdelaleem et al., 2025](#bib.bib11)\],
while successive-refinement formulations study information preservation
across multiple stages of compression \[[Charvin et al.,
2023](#bib.bib12)\]. Predictive State Representations (PSRs) instead
characterize state through conditional predictions of future observables
rather than reconstruction of the complete history \[[Littman et al.,
2001](#bib.bib43), [Singh et al., 2004](#bib.bib44)\]. These
perspectives establish task relevance and future predictability as
principled criteria for representation quality. Our setting introduces
an additional compositional requirement: the predictive distinctions
retained at one interface must remain sufficient for the predictive
requirements encountered at the next interface. We therefore do not
introduce another reconstruction or fixed future-prediction target; we
formulate a local predictive-sufficiency condition whose role is
explicitly tied to recursive composition.

Directly enforcing such a condition through full conditional
distributions or mutual information is generally impractical in
high-dimensional learned systems. Mutual-information estimation from
finite samples is statistically difficult, with variational estimators
exhibiting substantial bias–variance trade-offs \[[Poole et al.,
2019](#bib.bib13)\]. Predictive-state learning faces a related
approximation problem because practical systems must restrict attention
to a finite collection of predictive tests \[[Kulesza et al.,
2015](#bib.bib14)\]. Spectral PSRs, conditional-distribution embeddings
\[[Song et al., 2009](#bib.bib45)\], and conditional mean or
operator-regression methods \[[Grünewälder et al., 2012](#bib.bib46)\]
therefore characterize predictive relationships through finite tests,
regressions, or cross-covariance operators. Our empirical predictive
deficiency follows this computational philosophy, but the direction of
construction is important: the recursive predictive requirement is
specified first, and finite tests, ridge readouts, and covariance
balancing are introduced only as a tractable approximation to that
requirement. Thus, empirical predictive deficiency approximates but does
not define this preservation criterion.

A separate line of work concerns how preservation or compatibility
constraints are enforced during optimization. GEM constrains a current
gradient using losses on episodic memories from previous tasks and
projects violating gradients through a quadratic program \[[Lopez-Paz
and Ranzato, 2017](#bib.bib47)\]; A-GEM replaces the collection of
per-task constraints with a reference-gradient constraint \[[Chaudhry et
al., 2019](#bib.bib48)\]; and PCGrad modifies multi-task gradients
according to pairwise gradient conflicts \[[Yu et al.,
2020](#bib.bib49)\]. More generally, projected and proximal optimization
methods allow an optimizer step to be followed by a correction with
respect to a prescribed feasible domain, including adaptive methods for
constrained stochastic optimization \[[Duchi et al., 2011](#bib.bib52),
[Reddi et al., 2018](#bib.bib53)\]. We therefore do not regard gradient
projection, quadratic programming, or post-optimizer projection itself
as a contribution.

Our distinction lies instead in what defines the constrained update and
where that constraint is imposed. GEM-type methods derive admissible
directions from task losses or gradient compatibility, while
conventional projected optimization assumes a feasible parameter domain
specified independently of the current predictive state. In our setting,
the predictive-feasible region is generated dynamically from predictive
sensitivities of empirical predictive deficiency at recursive
interfaces. Moreover, because the first-order predictive constraint is
defined over an actual parameter displacement, we apply it to the task
proposal produced by the host optimizer rather than directly projecting
the raw task gradient before Adam or AdamW is applied. Given the task
proposal $`t_{t}`$, predictive feasibility projection returns the
closest constrained update $`d_{t}^{\star}`$ in the predictive-feasible
region $`\mathcal{F}_{t}`$. The optimization contribution is therefore
not a new projection operator, but the conversion of local predictive
sufficiency into data-dependent margin-relaxed predictive constraints on
the task proposal.

Taken together, prior work provides powerful mechanisms for
reconstruction, future prediction, information-theoretic compression,
predictive-state approximation, and constrained optimization. The
remaining question addressed here is different: what local condition
should an intermediate state satisfy when that state will itself be
aggregated, compressed, and reused in later computation? We formulate
this condition through local predictive sufficiency, approximate its
local predictive deficiency with empirical predictive deficiency, and
use predictive sensitivities to restrict updates that would erode the
corresponding predictive distinctions.

All four experimental domains follow the same high-level reproduction
path: data preparation, matched-host initialization, training of Ours,
validation- or development-only model selection, and frozen final
evaluation.

metrics, and evaluation cohorts;

documents the host-specific integration, optimization settings, compute
environment, and method-specific frozen hyperparameters. Exact
executable commands and frozen configuration files are provided with the
supplementary code release.
````
