````math
##### Execution order.

To reproduce a reported result, we first construct the corresponding
dataset and evaluation cohort using the domain-specific protocol in
Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Experimental Protocols and Evaluation Details ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
We then initialize or train the matched host model and train Ours from
the prescribed host state using the corresponding implementation in
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
Checkpoint or strategy selection uses only the validation or development
data specified for that domain. The selected state is subsequently
frozen and passed to the corresponding evaluation protocol without
further parameter updates or held-out-based selection. Metrics are
aggregated according to the rules specified in
Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Experimental Protocols and Evaluation Details ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),
and the complete run-level results are reported in
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
Training budgets, repetitions, software, and compute configurations are
summarized in
Appendix [E.5](#A5.SS5 "E.5 Training and Compute Configuration ‣ Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),
while the method-specific frozen hyperparameters are reported in
Appendix [E.8](#A5.SS8 "E.8 Method Hyperparameters and Sensitivity ‣ Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

### C.1 Temporal Graphs

##### Datasets and provenance.

We evaluate on Wikipedia \[[Kumar et al., 2019](#bib.bib26)\] and UCI
\[[Panzarasa et al., 2009](#bib.bib27)\], two established
continuous-time interaction networks. Wikipedia contains timestamped
interactions between users and Wikipedia pages together with dynamic
user-state labels, and is used for node classification. UCI contains
timestamped message exchanges between users in an online community and
is used for temporal link prediction.

We use the processed benchmark files released with DGB \[[Poursafaei et
al., 2022](#bib.bib28)\].²² 2 Dataset archive:
[https://zenodo.org/records/7213796](https://zenodo.org/records/7213796);
DGB code: [https://github.com/fpour/DGB](https://github.com/fpour/DGB).
Within each task, the same processed data are used for all compared
methods.
Table [4](#A3.T4 "Table 4 ‣ Datasets and provenance. ‣ C.1 Temporal Graphs ‣ Appendix C Experimental Protocols and Evaluation Details ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
summarizes the dataset statistics and evaluation settings.

|           |       |              |                     |       |
|-----------|-------|--------------|---------------------|-------|
| Dataset   | Nodes | Interactions | Task                | Depth |
| Wikipedia | 9,227 | 157,474      | Node classification | 2, 3  |
| UCI       | 1,899 | 59,835       | Link prediction     | 3, 4  |

Table 4: Temporal-graph datasets and evaluation settings.

##### Task and host selection.

The two datasets provide complementary prediction targets on
continuous-time interaction streams: Wikipedia evaluates node-level
state prediction, whereas UCI evaluates edge-level interaction
prediction. Together, they allow us to examine predictive preservation
across both prediction targets and multiple temporal depths.

We use TGN \[[Rossi et al., 2020](#bib.bib4)\] as the temporal-graph
host and as the corresponding uncompressed baseline. TGN combines
persistent node memory with temporal-neighborhood aggregation and
exposes intermediate representations across successive propagation
stages, providing natural recursive interfaces at which our method can
be applied. Comparing TGN with our method keeps the host architecture,
temporal encoding, and task definition fixed, thereby isolating the
effect of predictive preservation at intermediate representations.

##### Baselines and common protocol.

We compare against TGN \[[Rossi et al., 2020](#bib.bib4)\], TGAT \[[Xu
et al., 2020](#bib.bib3)\], TCL \[[Wang et al., 2021](#bib.bib30)\],
CTAN \[[Gravina et al., 2024](#bib.bib31)\], and DyGFormer \[[Yu et al.,
2023](#bib.bib5)\]. For reproducibility, baseline implementations follow
their publicly released reference codebases. We use the unified DyGLib
implementations \[[Yu et al., 2023](#bib.bib5)\] for TGN, TGAT, TCL, and
DyGFormer,³³ 3 DyGLib:
[https://github.com/yule-BUAA/DyGLib](https://github.com/yule-BUAA/DyGLib).
and the official CTAN implementation for CTAN.⁴⁴ 4 CTAN:
[https://github.com/gravins/non-dissipative-propagation-CTDGs](https://github.com/gravins/non-dissipative-propagation-CTDGs).
Where applicable, we use the accompanying Temporal Graph Benchmark (TGB)
utilities for temporal-graph data handling and evaluation.⁵⁵ 5 TGB:
[https://github.com/shenyangHuang/TGB](https://github.com/shenyangHuang/TGB).
Within each task, all methods use the same processed dataset,
chronological split, evaluation examples, and metric implementation.
Each baseline retains its native representation, propagation operator,
and architecture.

Interactions are ordered by timestamp and split at the 70th and 85th
timestamp percentiles, yielding chronological 70%/15%/15% training,
validation, and test partitions. No interaction from a later split is
used for parameter optimization in an earlier split.

Depth denotes the number of successive temporal representation or
propagation stages used by each model. We vary depth to examine how
predictive performance changes as historical information passes through
more successive representation transformations. The operation performed
at each stage remains architecture-specific; equal depth therefore
denotes a matched number of successive representation stages rather than
identical internal computation or an identical node-level receptive
field across different architectures.

For the TGN host, temporal neighborhoods are constructed causally using
only interactions preceding the query time. Wikipedia uses the 10 most
recent available interactions at depth 2 and 5 at depth 3, whereas UCI
uses 10 at depth 3 and 5 at depth 4. The smaller fanout in the deeper
settings limits the growth of recursively expanded temporal
neighborhoods as representation depth increases. Our method preserves
the host temporal encoding and temporal-neighbor selection rule and
introduces no additional source of future input information.

##### Wikipedia node classification.

For Wikipedia, we follow the standard TGN/JODIE dynamic
node-classification setting. A temporal representation model is first
trained on the interaction stream, after which a node-classification
head is optimized using binary cross-entropy on the observed dynamic
labels. Training, validation, and test examples follow disjoint
chronological splits.

Wikipedia is evaluated at depths 2 and 3. Checkpoint selection is based
on ROC-AUC on the held-out validation split. Because TGN maintains
persistent node memory, the temporal state required at the beginning of
test evaluation must reflect the interaction history preceding the test
period. After loading the selected checkpoint, we therefore clear the
memory and replay the training and validation interactions in
chronological order without parameter updates before evaluating on the
test stream. This procedure reconstructs only the causal memory state;
parameters remain fixed.

We report ROC-AUC on the complete held-out test split. Predictions and
labels from all evaluation mini-batches are first concatenated, and
ROC-AUC is then computed once over the full split. We do not average
independently computed mini-batch ROC-AUC values.

##### UCI temporal link prediction.

For UCI, we evaluate transductive temporal link prediction at depths 3
and 4. The main experiment uses the chronological transductive setting;
new-node subsets are not included in the reported results.

Training negatives are sampled by drawing destination nodes from the
unique destination-node pool observed in the training stream. Validation
and test evaluation use fixed random negative samplers over the
full-stream node pools, with sampler seeds 0 and 2, respectively. Each
positive interaction is paired with one negative interaction during
evaluation. The same positive examples and negative-sampling protocol
are used for all compared methods.

Model selection uses only the held-out validation split. The best
checkpoint is selected by full-validation average precision (AP), with
ROC-AUC recorded as an additional validation metric. The selected
checkpoint is then evaluated on the held-out test split.

We report AP and ROC-AUC for UCI. Predictions and labels from all
evaluation mini-batches are concatenated before either metric is
computed, so the reported values are global split-level metrics rather
than averages of mini-batch AP or ROC-AUC values.

All formal temporal-graph comparisons are repeated over five independent
random seeds.
Appendix [D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Temporal Graph Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
reports mean $`\pm`$ standard deviation across these runs. Evaluation
examples and the validation/test negative-sampling protocol are matched
across methods within each run.

|                        |                           |                           |
|------------------------|---------------------------|---------------------------|
| Setting                | Wikipedia                 | UCI                       |
| Task                   | Node classification       | Temporal link prediction  |
| Evaluation setting     | Chronological             | Transductive              |
| Split                  | 70/15/15                  | 70/15/15                  |
| Depth                  | 2, 3                      | 3, 4                      |
| TGN temporal neighbors | 10 (depth 2); 5 (depth 3) | 10 (depth 3); 5 (depth 4) |
| Evaluation negatives   | –                         | 1 per positive            |
| Checkpoint criterion   | Validation ROC-AUC        | Full-validation AP        |
| Reported metrics       | ROC-AUC                   | AP / ROC-AUC              |
| Metric aggregation     | Global                    | Global                    |

Table 5: Temporal-graph evaluation protocol.

### C.2 Language Memory

##### Datasets and tasks.

We evaluate language-memory compression on DailyDialog \[[Li et al.,
2017](#bib.bib29)\] and Multi-Session Chat (MSC) \[[Xu et al.,
2022](#bib.bib35)\]. DailyDialog is a multi-turn dialogue corpus and is
used for next-utterance language modeling. MSC is a multi-session
dialogue corpus and is used for long-term response prediction across
sessions.

For both datasets, we preserve their original dialogue structure and
chronological ordering. DailyDialog follows the data representation and
preprocessing of the released CCM implementation.⁶⁶ 6 Official CCM
implementation:
[https://github.com/snu-mllab/Context-Memory](https://github.com/snu-mllab/Context-Memory).
DailyDialog is obtained from the original release linked by the dataset
paper, and MSC is obtained through the public ParlAI task release.⁷⁷ 7
DailyDialog:
[https://aclanthology.org/I17-1099/](https://aclanthology.org/I17-1099/);
MSC: [https://parl.ai/projects/msc/](https://parl.ai/projects/msc/). For
MSC, conversation sessions are processed in chronological order so that
information accumulated from earlier sessions can be retained for
prediction in later sessions.

The two tasks expose complementary prediction interfaces over
accumulated context. DailyDialog varies the amount of preceding dialogue
history while holding the current context and prediction target fixed.
MSC evaluates response prediction across successive session depths as
the amount of available cross-session history increases.

##### Backbones and memory host.

We instantiate the language-memory experiments with two language-model
backbones: LLaMA-7B and google/gemma-4-E4B-it. CCM \[[Kim et al.,
2024](#bib.bib22)\] serves as the common recursive memory-compression
host across both backbones.

For a dialogue with recursively compressed history
$`(u_{1},\ldots,u_{L})`$, CCM updates its memory through the sequence of
interfaces

|     |                                                           |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       (M_{t-1},u_{t})\longrightarrow M_{t},\qquad t=1,\ldots,L,
       ```                                                        |     |

and we identify the post-compression state with

|     |                       |     |
|-----|-----------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       Z_{t}^{\Gamma}=M_{t}.
       ```                    |     |

We use $`Z_{t}^{\Gamma}`$ to denote the post-update memory state at this
recursive interface; the host-specific realization of $`\Gamma`$ is
described in
Appendix [E.2](#A5.SS2 "E.2 Language-Memory Implementation ‣ Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
Across both backbones, the base language-model parameters remain frozen
during RPBE adaptation, while the native compression parameters consist
of the conditional LoRA parameters in the attention projections together
with the trainable COMP/SUM embedding rows.

All matched comparisons within a backbone use the same raw task
examples, native tokenizer and serialization rule, recursive CCM memory
layout, and evaluation construction. Full-context and no-history
variants, when reported, use the same query and target and differ only
in the treatment of the preceding history. Detailed trainable-parameter,
predictive-statistic, and optimization configurations are reported in
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

##### DailyDialog protocol.

We preserve the original DailyDialog training, validation, and test
partitions and the text normalization used by CCM. Following the CCM
preprocessing, dialogues shorter than three utterances are removed,
individual utterances are limited to 128 tokens, with total token limits
of 400 in training and 600 in held-out evaluation.

To isolate the effect of recursive memory depth, we use a fixed-target
controlled-depth protocol. The evaluation cohort is restricted to
dialogues containing at least 15 utterances. For every evaluated depth,
the 15th utterance is used as the prediction target and the 14th
utterance as the current context. We vary only the number of immediately
preceding history utterances,

|     |                      |     |
|-----|----------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       L\in\{4,6,8,11,13\}.
       ```                   |     |

Thus, for a dialogue $`(u_{1},\ldots,u_{15})`$, the input at depth $`L`$
contains the $`L`$ utterances immediately preceding $`u_{14}`$ as the
recursively compressed history, followed by the fixed context
$`u_{14}`$, while the prediction target remains $`u_{15}`$.

The dialogue cohort, current context, prediction target, and scored
target positions are held fixed across depths. Consequently, variation
along the depth axis changes only the amount of history processed
through the recursive memory-compression mechanism. All matched methods
are evaluated on exactly the same dialogue instances, history depths,
context–target pairs, and scored target positions. Within each backbone,
the tokenizer, serialization rule, and recursive CCM memory construction
are also held fixed.

For the reported fixed-target depth analysis, the validation and test
partitions are combined after preprocessing to form a larger fixed
evaluation cohort. This increases the number of eligible long dialogues
and reduces sampling variability in the depth-wise comparison.
Checkpoint and hyperparameter selection are completed beforehand using
the pre-specified validation criterion; the pooled fixed-target scores
are never used for model selection. The same frozen cohort is used at
every depth and for every compared method.

We report dialogue-equal target perplexity. Prompt, history,
compression, padding, and terminal EOS positions are excluded from
scoring. For dialogue $`i`$ at depth $`L`$, let $`Y_{i}`$ denote its
scored target tokens. We first compute the mean target negative
log-likelihood

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\ell_{i,L}=\frac{1}{|Y_{i}|}\sum_{t\in Y_{i}}-\log p(y_{i,t}\mid\operatorname{input}_{i,L}),
``` |  |

and then aggregate dialogues with equal weight,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{PPL}^{\mathrm{dlg}}_{L}=\exp\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell_{i,L}\right).
``` |  |

This aggregation treats each dialogue as one evaluation unit and
prevents longer target utterances from receiving greater weight solely
because they contain more scored tokens. The same scoring and
aggregation rule is applied to every method in the matched comparison.

Because LLaMA-7B and Gemma-4 use different native tokenizers, absolute
perplexity values are compared within each backbone rather than across
backbone families.

##### MSC protocol.

We preserve the original MSC training, validation, and test partitions
\[[Xu et al., 2022](#bib.bib35)\] and maintain the chronological
ordering of sessions and utterances. Each conversation spans five
sessions, allowing information accumulated from earlier interactions to
remain available during later sessions.

For the depth-controlled evaluation, the same set of complete
five-session conversations is evaluated at the session openings

|     |                |     |
|-----|----------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       S\in\{3,4,5\}.
       ```             |     |

For session $`S`$, the preceding sessions $`(1,\ldots,S-1)`$ constitute
the available conversation history. The opening user utterance of
session $`S`$ is used as the current context, and the corresponding
response is used as the prediction target. Increasing $`S`$ therefore
increases the amount of accumulated cross-session history available
before prediction while retaining the same conversation cohort.

For the recursive comparison, CCM and Ours process the same ordered
conversation history through the CCM recursive merge host. The session
ordering, current context, prediction target, and evaluation examples
are matched within each setting, and no future-session information is
provided as input.

Checkpoint and hyperparameter selection are performed using the
pre-specified validation criterion before final evaluation. The selected
checkpoint is then frozen and evaluated on the same held-out test
conversations for all $`S`$.

We report dialogue-equal session-opening perplexity. Prompt, preceding
history, compression, padding, and terminal EOS positions are excluded
from scoring. For conversation $`i`$ at session depth $`S`$, let
$`Y_{i,S}`$ denote the scored target tokens of the opening response. We
first compute the mean target negative log-likelihood

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\ell_{i,S}=\frac{1}{|Y_{i,S}|}\sum_{t\in Y_{i,S}}-\log p(y_{i,S,t}\mid\operatorname{input}_{i,S}),
``` |  |

and then aggregate conversations with equal weight,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{PPL}^{\mathrm{dlg}}_{S}=\exp\!\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell_{i,S}\right).
``` |  |

The same set of complete conversations is evaluated at every session
depth, so $`N`$ is constant across $`S`$. This aggregation treats each
session-opening prediction as one evaluation unit and prevents longer
target responses from receiving greater weight solely because they
contain more scored tokens. All methods use identical scoring and
aggregation.

As with DailyDialog, absolute perplexity is compared within each
backbone, not across backbones.

##### Model selection and reporting.

For each backbone and dataset, we first select one CCM-merge host
checkpoint using the pre-specified validation criterion and then keep
this checkpoint fixed. The same checkpoint provides the reported
CCM-merge baseline and the common initialization for three independent
Ours training runs. Ours uses the same pre-specified training and
checkpoint-selection rule across the three seeds, and the selected
checkpoint from each run is evaluated unchanged over the complete depth
grid; no checkpoint or hyperparameter is selected per depth.

CCM-merge therefore contributes one fixed baseline result at each depth,
whereas Ours is reported as mean $`\pm`$ standard deviation over the
three independent training seeds. The relative PPL change is computed
against the same fixed CCM-merge baseline, with its reported variability
induced by the three Ours runs. Training seeds, checkpoint-selection
rules, optimization budgets, and trainable parameter counts are reported
in
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

For DailyDialog, the pooled fixed-target depth scores are computed only
after checkpoint selection and are used for the controlled depth
analysis. For MSC, the fixed CCM-merge checkpoint and the selected
checkpoint from each Ours run are evaluated on the held-out test
conversations over the complete session-depth grid $`S\in\{3,4,5\}`$.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
| Setting | DailyDialog | MSC |
| Task | Next-utterance prediction | Session-opening prediction |
| Backbones | LLaMA-7B / Gemma-4 | LLaMA-7B / Gemma-4 |
| Host | CCM-merge | CCM-merge |
| Depth | $`L=4,6,8,11,13`$ | $`S=3,4,5`$ |
| Evaluation cohort | Fixed pooled $`\geq 15`$-utterance dialogues | Fixed five-session test conversations |
| History | $`L`$ preceding utterances | Sessions $`1,\ldots,S-1`$ |
| Current context | Fixed $`u_{14}`$ | Opening user utterance of session $`S`$ |
| Prediction target | Fixed $`u_{15}`$ | Opening response of session $`S`$ |
| Metric | Dialogue-equal target PPL $`\downarrow`$ | Dialogue-equal session-opening PPL $`\downarrow`$ |
| Checkpoint selection |    CCM-M fixed once;  Ours validation-selected |    CCM-M fixed once;  Ours validation-selected |
| Depth evaluation |    Fixed CCM-M + each Ours checkpoint  over full $`L`$ grid |    Fixed CCM-M + each Ours checkpoint  over full $`S`$ grid |

Table 6: Language-memory evaluation protocol.

### C.3 Vision–Language–Action

##### Benchmark and data protocol.

We evaluate vision–language–action memory on the T3 task of LIBERO-Mem
\[[Chung et al., 2026](#bib.bib32)\]. T3 requires the robot to lift the
same bowl and return it to the plate three times in sequence, so
successful execution requires maintaining task progress across repeated
interactions.

LIBERO-Mem provides 100 refined T3 demonstrations for model development
in the released training data. We partition these into 80 demonstrations
for gradient-based training and 20 demonstrations used only as an
internal development split for checkpoint selection. No
development-split sample enters gradient updates.

After checkpoint selection, the selected checkpoint is frozen and
evaluated on the 20 official LIBERO-Mem validation initializations,
which are held out from both gradient training and checkpoint selection
and are used only for our final evaluation. These validation
initializations are disjoint from both the 80 training demonstrations
and the 20-demonstration internal checkpoint-selection split.

##### Pretrained initialization.

All runs start from the same OpenVLA-7B Prismatic
checkpoint,step-295000-epoch-40-loss=0.2200.pt, downloaded from
openvla/openvla-7b-prismatic. This checkpoint provides the pretrained
visual encoders, Llama-2 backbone, and multimodal projector. It does not
contain the LIBERO-Mem action model or memory modules used in our
experiments.

The parameters inherited from this pretrained checkpoint remain frozen.
MemoryVLA-specific components are initialized separately, including the
DiT-L action model (action_model), cognitive memory bank (cog_mem_bank),
perceptual memory bank (per_mem_bank), and perceptual compressor
(per_compr). The DiT-L action model predicts 7-dimensional actions over
a 16-step action horizon.

##### MemoryVLA host training.

We first train the MemoryVLA host using AVG as its memory aggregation
rule. During this stage, the trainable components are the LoRA adapters,
DiT action model, cog_mem_bank, per_mem_bank, and per_compr. The
original pretrained Llama-2, DINOv2, SigLIP, and multimodal-projector
parameters remain frozen.

We use --reg-head 0; therefore, no auxiliary regression head is
instantiated or trained.

##### Training Ours.

Ours is initialized from the trained MemoryVLA host of the same training
run. The complete host is then frozen, including its trained LoRA
adapters, DiT action model, cognitive memory bank, perceptual memory
bank, and perceptual compressor.

We introduce our compressor $`\Gamma`$, implemented as GammaMerger, and
optimize only its parameters using the predictive-preservation
objective. No existing host parameter is updated during this stage. We
additionally verified that the parameters shared by MemoryVLA and Ours
remain unchanged during Gamma-only training.

Thus, Ours does not receive additional fine-tuning of the underlying VLA
policy. The method-specific training stage only fits the newly
introduced memory-compression module on top of an otherwise fixed
MemoryVLA host.

##### Independent training runs.

We report results over three independently trained runs. For each run,
the MemoryVLA host is trained from the same pretrained OpenVLA
initialization using the procedure above, and Ours is then trained from
the corresponding host checkpoint with the complete host frozen and only
$`\Gamma`$ optimized. The reported mean and standard deviation use the
three independent training runs, irrespective of seed identifiers.

Within each run, Ours is initialized from the corresponding trained
MemoryVLA host, and the matched MemoryVLA baseline is evaluated from
that same host training run. The intervention is the memory-compression
mechanism and its predictive-preservation training.

##### Checkpoint selection.

All formal VLA runs use a maximum budget of 25,000 optimizer steps and
save checkpoints every 1,000 steps. Checkpoint selection is performed
exclusively on the 20-demonstration internal development split drawn
from the released refined training data. This split is used only for
selection, with no gradient updates.

Checkpoints are ranked by full-task success on the internal development
split, with SGC used only to break ties. Once selected, a checkpoint is
fixed before any rollout on the 20 official LIBERO-Mem validation
initializations.

The same checkpoint-selection rule and a maximum budget of 25,000
optimizer steps are applied to each method-specific training stage.
After one checkpoint has been selected for each training run, that
checkpoint is frozen before the official held-out evaluation is started.
The checkpoint-selection traces and selected checkpoints are reported in
Appendix [D.3](#A4.SS3 "D.3 Vision–Language–Action Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle");
the training configuration is summarized in
Appendix [E.5](#A5.SS5 "E.5 Training and Compute Configuration ‣ Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

##### Held-out matched MemoryVLA evaluation.

Within each training run, the selected MemoryVLA and Ours checkpoints
are frozen and evaluated on the same fixed set of 20 official LIBERO-Mem
validation initializations, with matched observation and action
interfaces, rollout budget, rollout settings, and LIBERO-Mem evaluator.
These validation rollouts are distinct from the internal
checkpoint-selection demonstrations.

For MemoryVLA, the host uses AVG aggregation. For Ours, the same host
parameters are kept fixed and AVG is replaced by the trained $`\Gamma`$
memory-compression module. This matched construction isolates the effect
of the proposed memory-compression method from additional adaptation of
the underlying policy.

The same fixed held-out validation initializations are used for matched
MemoryVLA–Ours comparisons across training runs. Reusing the benchmark
evaluation cases does not create additional training runs; the reported
uncertainty is computed across independently trained runs.

##### SR and SGC.

We report full-task success rate (SR) and sequential-goal completion
(SGC), both in percent. Each T3 rollout contains three sequential
lift-and-return repetitions.

Let $`N`$ denote the number of evaluation rollouts. For rollout $`i`$,
let $`s_{i}\in\{0,1\}`$ indicate whether all three repetitions are
successfully completed according to the LIBERO-Mem evaluator, and let
$`k_{i}\in\{0,1,2,3\}`$ denote the number of repetitions successfully
credited during that rollout. We compute

|     |                                               |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathrm{SR}=\frac{100}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}s_{i},
       ```                                            |     |

and

|     |                                                 |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathrm{SGC}=\frac{100}{3N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}k_{i}.
       ```                                              |     |

In our held-out evaluation, $`N=20`$, corresponding to one closed-loop
T3 rollout from each official LIBERO-Mem validation initialization.
Therefore, SR is the percentage of the 20 rollouts that successfully
complete all three repetitions, whereas SGC is the total number of
successfully completed repetitions divided by

|     |                |     |
|-----|----------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       3\times 20=60.
       ```             |     |

For example, a rollout completing one, two, or all three repetitions
contributes one, two, or three counts, respectively, to the SGC
numerator.

All repetition counting, stability checks, and overshoot handling use
the original LIBERO-Mem evaluator unchanged. We introduce no
method-specific success or partial-success criterion.

##### Prior-work references and comparison policy.

For broader context,
Table [1](#S4.T1 "Table 1 ‣ Models and baselines. ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
additionally includes the LIBERO-Mem results reported by Chung et al. 
\[[Chung et al., 2026](#bib.bib32)\] for $`\pi_{0}`$, SlotVLA with
$`h=1`$, SlotVLA with $`h=8`$, and E-SlotSSM. These values are
reproduced from the published study and are not re-evaluated under our
local MemoryVLA pipeline. We therefore treat them as contextual
reference points rather than protocol-matched baselines.

All claims concerning the effect of our method are based on the matched
MemoryVLA–Ours comparison described above. E-SlotSSM is marked with
$`\dagger`$ for using oracle subgoal information; it is an
oracle-assisted reference.

We preserve the quantities reported by the source wherever possible. If
a prior method reports zero sequential-goal completion, we also report
zero full-task success because completing none of the three required
repetitions cannot constitute successful completion of T3. Conversely,
partial sequential-goal completion does not determine full-task success,
so SR is left unreported when it cannot be obtained from the source.

Exact optimization hyperparameters, training budgets, checkpoint
intervals, software versions, and hardware configuration are provided in
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

### C.4 Self-Recursive Improvement

##### Purpose and evaluation setting.

We use self-recursive improvement (SRI) as a mechanism-level test of
predictive preservation under explicit recursive strategy refinement. In
this setting, previously generated execution traces are repeatedly
reused to construct later strategies, making the retained recursive
history directly relevant to subsequent computation. Our comparison is
therefore designed to isolate the effect of predictive preservation
while holding the realized initialization, capacity, recursive-search
opportunity, backbone, and evaluator fixed.

##### Benchmark, data, and released host.

We evaluate on a fixed six-task cohort from CO-Bench \[[Sun et al.,
2026](#bib.bib34)\]. CO-Bench provides task descriptions, development
instances, held-out test instances, and a common evaluator that maps
solver outputs to normalized task scores. We obtain the benchmark data
from the official CO-Bench release on Hugging Face,⁸⁸ 8
[https://huggingface.co/datasets/CO-Bench/CO-Bench](https://huggingface.co/datasets/CO-Bench/CO-Bench)
using the format and evaluator provided by the public CO-Bench
repository.⁹⁹ 9
[https://github.com/sunnweiwei/CO-Bench](https://github.com/sunnweiwei/CO-Bench)
No task instance is regenerated or custom development/test split
introduced; the official evaluator and scoring rule remain unchanged.

The recursive host is the public Meta^(n) implementation.¹⁰¹⁰ 10
[https://github.com/minnesotanlp/meta-n](https://github.com/minnesotanlp/meta-n)
We use its released CO-Bench integration (--benchmark co_bench) and
evolutionary archive orchestrator (--use-archive). The six-task cohort
is fixed before the paired comparison and is identical for Meta^(n) and
Ours. The exact task identifiers, repository revisions, and run-level
configurations are retained in the experiment manifest.

##### Backbones and API execution.

We evaluate three API backbones: gpt-5.5, gemini-3.1-pro,
andDeepSeek-V4.1-Flash. Within each backbone, Meta^(n) and Ours use the
same provider-side model identifier and the same API route. Following
the released host protocol, the same backbone is used for the Layer-1
solver and for every invocation of the recursive meta-operation
$`\Omega`$.

For each paired comparison, the provider route, decoding configuration,
maximum output length, retry policy, prompt templates, solver mode, and
execution timeout are frozen before recursive expansion and shared by
the two methods. Provider fallback is disabled, so failed requests
follow the same retry policy without silently switching to another model
or endpoint. Exact provider/model strings and decoding settings are
recorded in the run configuration.

##### Paired realized initialization.

Each Meta^(n)/Ours comparison begins from the same realized Layer-1
state. For a given backbone, the initial task programs, execution
traces, development scores, archive state, and associated metadata are
generated once and copied before the two recursive arms are expanded.
All locally controlled sources of randomness, including archive sampling
and task ordering, use paired settings across the two methods.

This design controls the realized API output at the point where the
methods begin to differ. Remote model calls need not be bitwise
deterministic, and independently regenerating the root would
simultaneously alter the initial programs, execution traces, and
downstream recursive environment. After the intervention begins, the two
recursive trajectories are allowed to diverge as a consequence of their
different historical-trace retention decisions; this downstream
divergence is part of the mechanism being evaluated.

##### Controlled predictive-preservation intervention.

Meta^(n) recursively refines candidate strategies using execution
information produced by earlier stages. In the paired comparison, both
methods use the same host procedure for constructing the available
execution-history pool, the same fixed retained-history capacity, the
same native solver stack and ordering convention, and the same
surrounding context construction.

Meta^(n) uses its fixed historical-trace retention rule. Ours applies
predictive preservation to determine which already available execution
traces are retained for the subsequent recursive update. The
predictive-preservation configuration is fixed before the paired
recursive search and receives no held-out information.

All remaining components are shared between the two arms: the CO-Bench
tasks, Layer-1 solver, $`\Omega`$ prompt and output schema, archive data
structure, outer parent-selection procedure, code executor, task
evaluator, and recursive-search machinery. Predictive preservation
operates only on information already produced within the host run and
creates no additional candidate proposal, evaluator query, retrieval
call, or external LLM request. The comparison therefore changes the
information retained at the recursive interface while preserving the
amount of retained history and the external opportunities available for
recursive search.

##### Matched recursive-search budget.

We use equal recursive-search opportunity as the primary resource
control. Before each paired run, a common archive-expansion budget is
fixed. Both methods receive the same number of candidate-generation
opportunities and the same evaluation allocation associated with each
candidate.

A candidate-generation slot is consumed once its generation has been
attempted. Parsing failures, invalid programs, execution errors, and
timeouts therefore remain part of the realized search cost and do not
receive free replacement proposals. The two arms also use the same
execution timeout, retry policy, maximum generation length, and maximum
number of host solver or repair calls associated with a candidate.

Method-dependent convergence statistics are retained as run diagnostics
but do not alter the number of recursive expansions available to either
method. Likewise, any difference in realized prompt length or token
usage is not converted into additional candidate generations, evaluator
calls, or model requests.

For resource accounting, we record candidate-generation attempts,
evaluator invocations, external LLM requests, input and output tokens,
and wall-clock time. Computation required internally by predictive
preservation is accounted for separately and does not enlarge the
external recursive-search budget.

##### Development-only recursive selection.

The recursive search uses only the development instances and development
feedback supplied by the official CO-Bench evaluator. Held-out scores
are not exposed to the Layer-1 solver, $`\Omega`$, historical-trace
retention, archive evolution, stopping logic, or predictive-preservation
configuration.

After the common recursive-search budget is exhausted, the
development-side archive, recursive lineage, and selected strategies are
frozen. We report the paper-facing *Dev Gain* under the same frozen
development-based selection protocol for Meta^(n) and Ours. The same
six-task development cohort, aggregation rule, evaluator, and score
scale are used in both arms.
Appendix [D.4](#A4.SS4 "D.4 Recursive Self-Improvement Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
reports cohort-level development results and search diagnostics.

Held-out results play no role in development-side candidate selection or
in the computation of Dev Gain.

##### Held-out evaluation.

Held-out evaluation begins only after the recursive search and
development-side selection have been frozen. The main table reports
held-out scores for tasks whose recursive strategy is modified during
the frozen development-side refinement process. The modified-task set is
identified from the recursive run artifacts before held-out scores are
examined.

For every task included in this set, the frozen Meta^(n) and Ours
solutions are evaluated on the same official held-out instances, and
their task scores are reported side by side. If several tasks are
modified for a backbone, all such tasks are retained in the comparison;
held-out performance is not used to choose among them.

The modified-task presentation in
Table [1](#S4.T1 "Table 1 ‣ Models and baselines. ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
highlights where the recursive intervention produces an actual
downstream strategy change. For completeness,
Appendix [D.4](#A4.SS4 "D.4 Recursive Self-Improvement Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
reports the task-wise held-out results for the full six-task cohort
together with the unconditional cohort-level averages for Meta^(n) and
Ours. Consequently, the complete held-out record does not depend on the
direction or magnitude of the main-table differences.

##### Repeated evaluation of frozen strategies.

For each backbone, we use one paired recursive-search realization
beginning from the shared realized Layer-1 state described above. Once
development-side selection is complete, the resulting strategies,
recursive configuration, prompt construction, model route, and held-out
task set are frozen.

Each frozen solution is evaluated three times on the same held-out
instances. Meta^(n) and Ours use the same backbone, provider route,
decoding configuration, instance set, instance ordering, retry policy,
and official evaluator in every repetition. The three-evaluation
protocol is fixed beforehand and recorded in the experiment manifest.

These repetitions quantify execution-time variability of an already
selected strategy under API stochasticity. They are repeated evaluations
of one frozen recursive-search outcome and are not treated as
independent recursive-search seeds. The main table reports the mean
held-out score over the three matched evaluations.
Appendix [D.4](#A4.SS4 "D.4 Recursive Self-Improvement Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
reports one fixed held-out evaluation for the complete six-task cohort
and a compact repeat-level variability summary.

##### Reporting and bias control.

The SRI protocol is fixed before held-out evaluation. The task cohort,
realized Layer-1 initialization, history-pool construction,
retained-history capacity, recursive-search budget, failure accounting,
development-side selection procedure, modified-task identification rule,
and repeated held-out evaluation protocol are all determined without
access to held-out outcomes.

Dev Gain and Held-out Score serve separate roles. Dev Gain measures the
result of recursive refinement under the feedback available during
search. Held-out Score evaluates the frozen resulting strategies on
unseen instances. Both are computed from the common CO-Bench evaluator
and neither is defined from the internal predictive statistic.

The paper-facing SRI comparison is limited to these task-performance
quantities and resource-accounting statistics. We do not introduce
additional post-hoc thresholds or method-specific evaluation scores into
the comparison. Main-table modified-task results are accompanied by the
complete six-task results and unconditional cohort-level averages in the
appendix.

|  |  |
|----|----|
| Component | Protocol |
| Data source | Official CO-Bench release |
| Task cohort | Same fixed six tasks |
| Host | Public Meta^(n) implementation |
| Backbones | gpt-5.5 / gemini-3.1-pro / DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash |
| Model assignment | Same backbone at Layer 1 and all $`\Omega`$ calls |
| Initialization | Same realized Layer-1 state within each pair |
| History-pool construction | Same host procedure |
| Retained-history capacity | Same fixed capacity in both arms |
| Native stack | Same construction and ordering |
| Method-specific change | Historical-trace retention rule only |
| Recursive-search procedure | Same host procedure |
| Search budget | Same fixed expansion budget |
| Candidate failures | Consume the assigned search slot |
| Evaluator | Official CO-Bench evaluator, unchanged |
| Search feedback | Development only |
| Held-out access during search | None |
| Additional API calls for Ours | None |
| Search realizations | One paired realization per backbone |
| Development result | Dev Gain $`\uparrow`$ |
| Held-out evaluation | Three matched evaluations of each frozen solution |
| Main-table held-out rows | Three-evaluation mean for all development-defined modified tasks |
| Appendix held-out results | One fixed evaluation for all six tasks + unconditional average |
| Resource accounting | Candidates / evals / calls / tokens / wall-clock |

Table 7: Controlled SRI protocol on CO-Bench. Meta^(n) and Ours share
the realized initialization, retained-history capacity, recursive-search
opportunity, and evaluation protocol; the method-specific intervention
changes which execution traces are retained for subsequent recursive
refinement.

##### Run artifacts and reproducibility.

We retain the native Meta^(n) artifacts for both arms, including
config.json, summary.json, convergence records, checkpoint.json,
archive/index.json, per-candidate execution traces and injected-code
records, and the JSONL log of external LLM requests and responses.

The manifest also records the six CO-Bench task identifiers, CO-Bench
and Meta^(n) repository revisions, exact provider/model identifiers,
decoding and retry settings, paired local randomness configuration,
common recursive-search budget, evaluator timeout, history-pool
configuration, retained-history capacity, historical-trace retention
settings, development-selected strategies, modified-task set, and
repeated held-out evaluation settings.

The minimal public data and software setup is:

``` ltx_verbatim
# CO-Bench data
huggingface-cli download CO-Bench/CO-Bench \
    --repo-type dataset --local-dir data/co_bench

# Meta^n host
git clone https://github.com/minnesotanlp/meta-n
cd meta-n
python -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate
pip install -e ".[bench]"
````

API-backed runs use the exact provider/model identifiers stored in the
frozen run configuration. Authentication is supplied through environment
variables and is not serialized into released experiment artifacts.
Paper-facing commands are generated from the frozen configuration rather
than edited separately between Meta^(n) and Ours.

Together, these artifacts reconstruct the common realized
initialization, method-specific recursive lineages, development-side
selection, and repeated held-out evaluation.

## Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses

This appendix reports complete results and domain-specific diagnostics
for the main comparisons. Temporal graphs include five-seed results and
predictive-constraint diagnostics; language memory includes the full
depth-wise perplexity comparisons and training-time predictive
diagnostics; VLA includes internal checkpoint-selection, held-out
initialization-level, and rollout analyses together with the three-run
aggregate; and SRI includes the complete six-task results, recursive
candidate and strategy evolution, and held-out variability.

Figure 5: Treewise predictive-constraint conflict during training.
Violation density is the fraction of grouped predictive constraints
violated by the unconstrained task proposal. On UCI 3-hop (R0,
$`\kappa=0.05`$), the mean violation density decreases from $`0.020`$ in
the first third of training to $`0.012`$ in the final third.

Figure [5](#A4.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ D.1 Temporal Graph Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
shows that predictive-constraint conflicts remain present throughout
training but become less frequent in later epochs. The trajectory is not
monotonic: conflict density rises temporarily in the middle of training
before declining, indicating that task optimization continues to propose
directions that can interfere with predictive preservation as the
representation evolves. The lower density in the final third suggests
that the task proposal becomes progressively more aligned with the
predictive-feasible region, reducing the amount of correction required
from the projection. This trend is consistent with the compressor and
task objective becoming better coordinated over training, while the
remaining violations motivate retaining the predictive constraints
rather than assuming that such conflicts disappear entirely.

|            |                            |                                      |                                                              |                  |
| ---------- | -------------------------- | ------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------- |
| $`\alpha`$ | $`\alpha\,q^{\top}\delta`$ | $`\Delta\widehat{J}_{\mathrm{win}}`$ | $`\Delta\widehat{J}_{\mathrm{win}}/(\alpha q^{\top}\delta)`$ | Relative scaling |
| 1          | $`3.639\times 10^{-7}`$    | $`4.044\times 10^{-7}`$              | $`1.111`$                                                    | $`1.0\times`$    |
| 16         | $`5.822\times 10^{-6}`$    | $`6.386\times 10^{-6}`$              | $`1.097`$                                                    | $`15.8\times`$   |

Table 9: Real-data first-order bridge audit on UCI. We compare the
first-order change predicted by the constraint surrogate with the
measured finite change of the exact window statistic used by
PairKFWindow. The perturbation scale $`\alpha`$ multiplies the same
parameter-space update direction.

##### Interpretation.

Table [9](#A4.T9 "Table 9 ‣ D.1 Temporal Graph Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
directly tests the local approximation used to construct our predictive
constraints. Here, $`q=\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{J}_{\mathrm{win}}`$
denotes the local predictive sensitivity represented by the constraint
surrogate, $`\delta`$ is the parameter-space perturbation direction, and
$`\Delta\widehat{J}_{\mathrm{win}}`$ uses the same PairKFWindow
tree-weighted window aggregation.

The predicted and measured changes have the same sign and closely
matched magnitudes. At the base perturbation scale, the measured change
is only $`1.111\times`$ the first-order prediction; after increasing the
perturbation by a factor of $`16`$, this ratio remains nearly unchanged
at $`1.097`$. More importantly, the predicted change grows by exactly
$`16.0\times`$, while the measured change grows by $`15.8\times`$. Thus,
over this resolvable local range, the empirical window statistic
responds almost linearly to the perturbation and is well tracked by the
directional quantity used in the constraint construction.

This audit is important because the optimization procedure does not
directly constrain the finite post-update value of
$`\widehat{J}_{\mathrm{win}}`$. Instead, it constrains its local
first-order surrogate through terms of the form $`q^{\top}\delta`$. The
close agreement above therefore verifies, on a real UCI training window,
the practical bridge between the predictive statistic used in the
implementation and the linearized half-space constraints imposed on
parameter updates. The remaining approximately $`10\%`$ scale difference
is stable across perturbation sizes rather than increasing with
$`\alpha`$, and therefore does not exhibit the growth expected from a
dominant nonlinear higher-order error.

### D.2 Language Memory Results

We report the matched CCM-merge–Ours comparison on DailyDialog and
Multi-Session Chat (MSC) across LLaMA-7B and Gemma-4-E4B-It. DailyDialog
uses the fixed-target controlled-depth protocol described in
Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Language Memory ‣ Appendix C Experimental Protocols and Evaluation Details ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),
while MSC evaluates session-opening prediction as the amount of
preceding cross-session history increases.

Within each backbone and dataset, CCM-merge and Ours use the same
evaluation examples, memory-depth construction, tokenizer, prediction
targets, and perplexity aggregation. The CCM-merge host checkpoint is
selected once by the pre-specified validation criterion and then kept
fixed. Ours is initialized from this same host checkpoint and trained
under three independent seeds. The selected checkpoint from each Ours
run is evaluated unchanged across the reported depth grid. Absolute
perplexity values are interpreted only within the same backbone because
LLaMA-7B and Gemma-4-E4B-It use different native tokenizers.

````math
reports dialogue-equal target PPL under the fixed-target

mean $`\pm`$ standard deviation of the seed-wise relative PPL change
with respect to the fixed CCM-merge baseline.

##### Training-time predictive diagnostics.

Beyond the final depth-wise results, we examine whether the
predictive-preservation signal retained across the recursive memory
interfaces evolves consistently with downstream validation performance
during training. For the internal diagnostic, $`J`$ denotes the
finite-test predictive value used in the main mechanism analysis, and we
compute $`\Delta J=J_{\mathrm{real}}-J_{\mathrm{shuffled}}`$ at every
optimization window, where $`J_{\mathrm{real}}`$ uses the correctly
paired future observations and $`J_{\mathrm{shuffled}}`$ breaks this
correspondence. We additionally report
$`\Delta J_{\mathrm{within}\text{-}L}`$, where shuffling is restricted
to examples at the same recursive depth, thereby controlling for
depth-dependent effects. Because the per-optimization-window statistic
is noisy, we report raw measurements and a $`w=9`$ smoothed trajectory.

Figure [6](#A4.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ Training-time predictive diagnostics. ‣ D.2 Language Memory Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
compares these internal predictive-preservation diagnostics with the
validation trajectory. The right panel reports the negative mean
relative PPL change of Ours with respect to the fixed CCM-merge host
baseline, averaged over all evaluated depths, so both panels follow the
same convention that higher values indicate better behavior. The
predictive-preservation signals remain positive throughout training,
while the validation improvement also remains above the CCM-merge
baseline. More importantly, their training dynamics are broadly aligned:
the smoothed $`\Delta J`$ signals strengthen through the intermediate
stage and reach their strongest region around the later-middle windows,
while the validation gain similarly increases toward s40; both
subsequently weaken toward s50. This shared trajectory links the
internal predictive-preservation signal to the observed downstream
improvement and provides supporting evidence for the validation-selected
checkpoint.

initialization–training-run instances, and report the three-run mean
$`\pm`$ standard deviation.

We provide additional results for recursive self-improvement (SRI),
including task-level held-out performance, candidate evolution during
recursive search, and a qualitative trace of the solver strategies
generated across successive rounds. The experiments test whether
predictive preservation changes the information propagated through
recursive improvement and, consequently, the downstream solver
trajectory. All three backbones follow the same SRI protocol described
in
````
