````math
##### Candidate evolution.

The final task scores alone do not show whether predictive preservation
actually changes the recursive search trajectory. We therefore inspect
the development-score trajectory for DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash. The two
procedures begin from the same depth-1 state but retain different
numbers of candidates and reach different scores.

|            |          |        |         |        |                 |
|------------|----------|--------|---------|--------|-----------------|
|            | Meta^(n) |        | Ours    |        |                 |
| Depth      | \#Cand.  | Dev    | \#Cand. | Dev    | $`\bm{\Delta}`$ |
| d1         | 1        | 0.6845 | 1       | 0.6845 | 0.0000          |
| d2 (merge) | 5        | 0.8073 | 5       | 0.8605 | +0.0532         |
| d3         | 6        | 0.8073 | 14      | 0.8546 | +0.0473         |
| d4         | 14       | 0.8037 | 4       | 0.8605 | +0.0568         |
| d5         | –        | –      | 2       | 0.8546 | –               |

Table 15: Candidate evolution for DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash. \#Cand. denotes
the number of retained candidates at each recursive depth. Ours changes
both the retained candidate set and the resulting development
trajectory.

##### Qualitative evolution of solver strategies.

To examine how the recursive context changes subsequent model responses,
we trace the gemini-3.1-pro recursive-search run across generations.
Table [16](#A4.T16 "Table 16 ‣ Qualitative evolution of solver strategies. ‣ D.4 Recursive Self-Improvement Results ‣ Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
summarizes the main solver strategies proposed by the model. The
clearest trajectory occurs on aircraft_landing, where the model
progressively separates discrete sequence search from continuous
landing-time optimization.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  Gen. |  Meta^(n) response |  Ours response |  Reason for the change |
|  1 |  Accelerate assignment solving and refine assortment packing through restarts and stock selection. |  Use linear_sum_assignment for assignment; diversify assortment ordering and stock selection. |  Runtime is the main limitation for assignment, while assortment is sensitive to fixed ordering and stock choices. |
|  2 |  Continue assortment refinement using stronger packing heuristics and stock combinations; one branch explores warehouse search. |  Remove restrictive assortment choices, improve optional-piece handling, and introduce MaxRects-style packing. |  Earlier generated solvers do not fully realize the intended packing strategy, leaving implementation-level restrictions. |
|  3 |  Continue assortment improvement with randomized ordering, GRASP-style search, and richer placement heuristics. |  Continue assortment refinement, introduce local search for warehouse, and apply simulated annealing (SA) to aircraft landing sequences. |  After assortment improves, the model identifies additional headroom in warehouse and aircraft optimization. |
|  4 |  Further refine assortment using SA/hill climbing and additional placement and stock-selection heuristics. |  Strengthen warehouse subset search and add exact transportation-LP evaluation; continue improving assortment packing helpers. |  Heuristic warehouse evaluation can mis-rank candidate subsets, motivating exact final evaluation. |
|  5 |  Continue assortment-oriented search and packing refinement. |  For aircraft_landing, retain SA for sequence search, keep the top 10 candidate sequences, and optimize landing times with scipy.optimize.linprog. |  The model identifies greedy landing-time assignment as the main remaining bottleneck: promising sequences can be mis-scored because early/late penalties are not optimized globally. |
|  6 |  Continue assortment refinement and implementation cleanup. |  Explore wider aircraft candidate funnels and exact-LP local refinement, while continuing assortment helper integration. |  After the Gen. 5 aircraft improvement, the model attributes the remaining limitation to candidate pre-selection before exact evaluation. |

Table 16: Evolution of model-generated solver strategies in
gemini-3.1-pro. Each row summarizes the main strategy proposed at one
generation and the model’s rationale for the subsequent change. The
deployed aircraft solver ultimately retains the Gen. 5 strategy:
simulated annealing over landing sequences followed by exact LP
refinement of the top candidate sequences.

The aircraft trajectory illustrates a progressive solver refinement. The
model first strengthens discrete search over landing sequences, then
identifies greedy landing-time assignment as the remaining bottleneck,
and finally introduces exact LP refinement over the best SA candidates.
Although later responses explore wider candidate funnels, the selected
deployed solver retains the Gen. 5 SA–plus–LP strategy.

##### Held-out evaluation variability.

Each main-table SRI held-out score is the mean over three matched
evaluations of the same frozen strategy. As a representative example,
the three held-out evaluations of the frozen Ours gemini-3.1-pro
aircraft strategy score $`0.9065`$, $`0.9259`$, and $`0.9378`$, yielding
a mean of 0.9234. The solver code, prompt, task instances, and
evaluation settings are fixed across repeats; these repetitions quantify
evaluation variability of one recursive-search outcome rather than
independent search seeds.

## Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility

This appendix describes how the common predictive-preservation framework
is instantiated in each host system, followed by controlled ablations
and hyperparameter sensitivity. Dataset construction, split rules, and
evaluation protocols are kept in
Appendix [C](#A3 "Appendix C Experimental Protocols and Evaluation Details ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle");
complete results and diagnostic analyses are kept in
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Extended Results and Diagnostic Analyses ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

### E.1 Temporal-Graph Implementation

##### Host recursion and recursive interfaces.

We use TGN as the common host for UCI temporal link prediction and
Wikipedia node classification. For a temporal query with propagation
depth $`L`$, TGN recursively constructs node representations from
temporally sampled neighborhoods. At each propagation layer, information
collected from the current temporal subtree is aggregated into an
intermediate representation and then reused by the parent recursion.
Consequently, signals originating deeper in the temporal neighborhood
must pass through more successive recursive interfaces before reaching
the task prediction. These reused intermediate representations are the
recursive interfaces at which predictive preservation is applied:
information needed by later prediction must survive the current
aggregation before it can propagate further toward the root.

##### Method insertion.

We instantiate the recursive interface at each internal propagation
layer $`0<\ell<L`$. TGN first computes its original temporal aggregation
at layer $`\ell`$. The compression map $`\Gamma`$ then receives the
layer-specific own state together with this vanilla aggregate and
produces the representation passed to the parent recursion. The leaf
layer ($`\ell=0`$) retains the raw host state, and the task-facing root
($`\ell=L`$) retains the original TGN aggregation operator. The method
therefore acts precisely on intermediate representations that will
subsequently be reused at another recursive interface after a further
round of temporal aggregation.

This construction is shared by both temporal-graph tasks. The host
temporal memory, neighborhood construction, temporal aggregation
procedure, and task pipeline are retained; the distinction between UCI
and Wikipedia remains the original benchmark task and evaluation
protocol.

##### Predictive records and predictive feasibility optimization.

During the same recursive query, the host adapter traces the eligible
internal interfaces together with the child–parent relationships
actually consumed by the TGN computation. These traces provide the
aligned local predictive records used to estimate the finite-test
predictive value and empirical predictive deficiency introduced in
Section [3](#S3 "3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
The resulting predictive sensitivities are used by the common
constrained-update procedure, with the predictive correction restricted
to the compression parameter block $`\Gamma`$. All remaining trainable
TGN parameters continue to follow the host task optimization.

The statistical configuration, hyperparameter selection, and
optimization settings associated with the predictive-preservation
procedure are reported jointly with the corresponding method-level
configurations below.

### E.2 Language-Memory Implementation

##### Host and recursive memory.

We use CCM as the host for the language-memory experiments. Under its
merge_recur construction, dialogue history is compressed progressively
rather than presented in full at every prediction step. After processing
the first $`t`$ history turns, CCM aggregates the corresponding
compression-token states into a compact memory $`M_{t}`$, which is then
reused when the next turn is incorporated. The resulting computation
therefore forms a chain

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
M_{1}\rightarrow M_{2}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow M_{L}\rightarrow\text{response prediction}.
``` |  |

The language-model backbone, dialogue objective, and decoding procedure
remain those of the CCM host.

##### Recursive interface and method insertion.

This recursive memory update instantiates the recursive interface used
by our method. Information from an early dialogue turn can influence the
final response only if it survives the intermediate memory states
through which the history is repeatedly propagated. In the notation of
the main method, the accumulated information available before the
current memory update plays the role of the pre-compression state
$`U_{t}`$, while the memory reused by subsequent CCM computation is the
compressed representation associated with $`Z_{t}^{\Gamma}`$.

Let $`\bar{M}_{t}`$ denote the memory obtained from the native CCM merge
at turn $`t`$. We retain this host computation and realize the
method-specific compression map through the residual $`R_{\Gamma}`$ at
the recursive merge interface:

|     |                                                                  |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       M_{t}^{\Gamma}=\bar{M}_{t}+R_{\Gamma}(M_{t-1}^{\Gamma},h_{t},t),
       ```                                                               |     |

where $`h_{t}`$ is the current compressed-turn representation. The
residual map is initialized with zero output, so the initial computation
coincides with the corresponding CCM host. The resulting
$`M_{t}^{\Gamma}`$ is then consumed by the next CCM update in the same
position as the original memory state.

##### Predictive observations.

For a dialogue

|     |                           |     |
|-----|---------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       [u_{1},\ldots,u_{L},c,y],
       ```                        |     |

we construct predictive observations at each eligible recursive memory
interface. For an intermediate cut, the predictive observations are
drawn from utterances that remain downstream of that memory state in the
same dialogue trajectory, combining a nearby future event with the
eventual response target. At the terminal cut, the remaining context
$`c`$ and response $`y`$ provide the final predictive observation. Thus,
every predictive observation is formed from information that is
genuinely unavailable before the corresponding compression event and
subsequently contributes to the same dialogue prediction.

A fixed readout of $`M_{t}^{\Gamma}`$ provides the compressed
representation used to estimate the finite-test predictive value. The
readout carries no trainable parameters and serves only to evaluate
predictive preservation at the recursive interface.

##### Training integration.

The same predictive-preservation criterion is evaluated at these
recursive interfaces, while language-memory optimization uses the
aggregate predictive update. This is a specialization of the same
training principle in which predictive preservation is enforced directly
on $`\Gamma`$, rather than through conflict-triggered projection of the
host update. The task gradient updates the trainable CCM compression
parameters together with $`\Gamma`$, whereas the predictive gradient is
restricted to $`\Gamma`$; the base language-model parameters remain
frozen. Consequently, the intervention changes how predictive
information is preserved across recursive memory updates without
changing the dialogue task or evaluation protocol. At inference, the
learned $`\Gamma`$ residual remains part of the recursive memory update,
while the training-time observer and predictive statistic are absent.
Optimization coefficients and formal training configurations are
reported separately with the shared hyperparameter settings.

### E.3 Vision–Language–Action Implementation

##### Host and recursive memory.

We instantiate the method on MemoryVLA for the LIBERO-Mem experiments.
MemoryVLA maintains a compact perceptual–cognitive memory that is
updated and reused throughout sequential visuomotor execution. At each
stage, the current visual observation, language instruction, and
previously retained memory are integrated to form the working state used
for subsequent memory updates and action generation. The resulting
computation therefore contains the same recursive structure as the
preceding domains: information acquired at an earlier stage must survive
a sequence of memory updates before it can influence later actions.

##### Recursive interface and method insertion.

The recursive working-memory update instantiates the recursive interface
used by our method. Let $`U_{v}`$ denote the working representation
available before the memory compression at stage $`v`$. We apply the
compression map at this interface,

|     |                               |     |
|-----|-------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       Z_{v}^{\Gamma}=\Gamma(U_{v}),
       ```                            |     |

and feed the resulting compact memory back into the original MemoryVLA
computation. This memory is subsequently reused by later perception,
memory-update, and action-generation stages. Hence, information
discarded at one interface cannot contribute to downstream control
decisions, making this recursive memory state the recursive interface at
which predictive preservation is applied.

The intervention is restricted to this internal recursive interface. The
original visual and language encoders, action-generation pathway, task
objective, and sequential execution procedure are otherwise retained.

##### Predictive observations.

Predictive observations are constructed from future events along the
same demonstration trajectory. For each eligible recursive memory
interface, the compressed state is paired with recorded downstream
actions that occur after that memory state has been formed. These future
actions instantiate the local predictive events used by the empirical
predictive criterion: a memory state is favored when it retains
information useful for later control.

The future trajectory is used only to construct training-time predictive
supervision. It is never provided to the working memory, compression
map, or policy input, and test-time rollouts follow causal MemoryVLA
execution.

##### Training integration.

The resulting interface-wise predictive records instantiate the same
predictive-preservation criterion used for temporal graphs and language
memory. The task loss continues to optimize the original VLA objective,
while the predictive-preservation signal constrains updates to the
designated memory compression block. Thus, the method changes how
information is retained across successive memory updates while leaving
the host task and downstream control computation intact.

The reported LIBERO-Mem experiments use the T3 setting throughout. The
predictive-preservation construction is applied unchanged across the
repeated stages of this multi-stage manipulation trajectory. Formal runs
use dense frame-level training with batch size 4 and gradient
accumulation 4 (effective batch 16), a constant schedule with zero
warmup, a task-LR argument of $`10^{-5}`$ and a Gamma learning rate of
$`2\times 10^{-5}`$. The host is frozen in the Gamma-only stage, so only
the Gamma optimizer updates method-specific parameters. All formal VLA
runs are capped at 25k optimizer steps and save a checkpoint every 1k
steps. Checkpoint selection and final held-out rollout evaluation use
disjoint data as specified in
Appendix [C.3](#A3.SS3 "C.3 Vision–Language–Action ‣ Appendix C Experimental Protocols and Evaluation Details ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle");
the official evaluation rollouts are not reused for model selection.

### E.4 Self-Recursive Improvement Implementation

##### Host recursion and recursive interfaces.

We instantiate the method on Meta^(n), where recursive improvement
proceeds by repeatedly reusing execution experience from earlier search
rounds. Layer-1 candidates produce task-solving programs and execution
traces; at subsequent depths, the operator $`\Omega`$ consumes retained
traces together with previously injected code to generate or refine the
next candidate. The resulting candidate is evaluated and may contribute
further experience to later recursive rounds.

Before every generate or refine call, Meta^(n) reduces the available
historical context to satisfy a fixed context budget. This
context-reduction step instantiates the recursive interface. Earlier
heuristics, execution outcomes, failure modes, and injected repair
routines can influence deeper search only if they survive this
reduction. Repeated selection therefore creates the same preservation
problem as repeated state compression in the preceding domains.

##### Offline lineage construction.

Unlike the other hosts, Meta^(n) does not expose a differentiable
recursive state together with its future observations during a single
forward pass. We therefore construct the predictive supervision from
completed host trajectories. We first execute Meta^(n) with its native
context reduction and freeze the resulting archives. From each archive,
we recover only lineages that actually occur in the recursive search,

|     |                                                      |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       c_{v}\longrightarrow c_{v+1}\longrightarrow c_{v+2}.
       ```                                                   |     |

Each candidate $`c_{v}`$ defines one recursive interface. Its
pre-compression state is

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
U_{v}=\begin{cases}(\text{traces},\,\text{context stack}),&\texttt{generate},\\[5.69054pt]
(\text{child traces},\,\text{context stack}+\text{previous injection}),&\texttt{refine}.\end{cases}
``` |  |

The remaining task-level prompt information is retained as $`C_{v}`$ and
is passed through unchanged.

Only real child and grandchild states are used to construct future
observations. For an eligible lineage, we define

|     |                                                     |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       Y_{v+1}=\Phi(T_{v+1}),\qquad Y_{v+2}=\Phi(T_{v+2}),
       ```                                                  |     |

where $`T_{v+1}`$ and $`T_{v+2}`$ are the execution traces produced by
the actual child and grandchild candidates. Interfaces without an
observed grandchild are excluded from the predictive record.

##### Historical-trace compression and retention.

The items contained in $`U_{v}`$ are encoded by a frozen encoder
$`E_{0}`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
X_{v}=\bigl[E_{0}(x_{1}),\ldots,E_{0}(x_{n_{v}})\bigr],\qquad q_{v}=E_{0}(\text{current traces}),
``` |  |

and passed to the learned compression map $`\Gamma_{\theta}`$. In the
implementation, $`\Gamma_{\theta}`$ uses a query-conditioned slotted
fusion:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
Q_{v}=Q_{0}+B_{c}A_{c}q_{v},\qquad K=X_{v}W_{K},\quad V=X_{v}W_{V},\quad g=\sigma(X_{v}w_{g}),
``` |  |

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
A_{v}=\operatorname{softmax}\left(\frac{Q_{v}K^{\top}}{\sqrt{d_{e}}}+\mathbf{1}\log(g+\epsilon)^{\top}+M_{v}\right),
``` |  |

|     |                                        |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       Z_{v}=\operatorname{LN}(Q_{v}+A_{v}V).
       ```                                     |     |

Here $`Z_{v}`$ is the compact representation used by the predictive
statistic, while $`A_{v}`$ determines which host-produced historical
items are retained. This is the discrete-retention specialization of the
same predictive- preservation principle used for continuous recursive
states: rather than controlling which components survive in a continuous
latent representation, $`\Gamma_{\theta}`$ controls which history items
survive the capacity-limited recursive interface. The reducer returns
the original Trace and InjectedCode objects selected under the same
context budget as Meta^(n); it does not synthesize additional experience
or modify the downstream prompt construction.

##### Predictive supervision and offline training.

The future side of each training record is obtained from the same
recovered lineage. We encode the real child and grandchild observations
as

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
f_{v}^{(1)}=E_{0}(\operatorname{serialize}(Y_{v+1})),\qquad f_{v}^{(2)}=E_{0}(\operatorname{serialize}(Y_{v+2})),
``` |  |

and form fixed predictive tests

|     |                                                     |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       p_{v}^{(r)}=R_{S}^{(r)}\begin{bmatrix}f_{v}^{(1)}\\
       f_{v}^{(2)}\end{bmatrix}.
       ```                                                  |     |

We additionally record the realized two-step improvement

|     |                                                                  |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       R_{v}=\operatorname{score}(c_{v+2})-\operatorname{score}(c_{v}).
       ```                                                               |     |

After projecting the compressed state to the statistic space,

|     |                                         |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       b_{v}=P_{b}\,\operatorname{vec}(Z_{v}),
       ```                                      |     |

the predictive and improvement statistics are

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
J_{\mathrm{LPSE}}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{r=1}^{4}J_{\mathrm{CCA}}(B,P^{(r)}),\qquad J_{\mathrm{task}}=J_{\mathrm{CCA}}(B,R).
``` |  |

Their sensitivities instantiate the same predictive-preservation
training principle used elsewhere in the paper: the task objective
favors histories associated with subsequent recursive improvement, while
the predictive-preservation term discourages discarding information that
remains predictive across the recovered interfaces.

Training of $`\Gamma_{\theta}`$ is performed entirely offline on the
frozen archive records. The language model and item encoder remain
fixed, and no additional $`\Omega`$ calls are made during this
optimization.

##### Evaluation-time integration.

After offline training, $`\Gamma_{\theta}`$ is frozen and inserted only
at the two native context-reduction points preceding generate and
refine. Formal evaluation uses only the information available at the
current recursive state; child and grandchild observations are used
solely for constructing the offline training records and are never
accessed during evaluation.

The Meta^(n) archive, parent-selection and acceptance rules, recursive
candidate-generation procedure, $`\Omega`$ prompt and output schema,
code executor, and evaluator remain unchanged. Meta^(n) and Ours use the
same context budget and recursive-search budget. Thus, SRI does not
introduce a different preservation objective; it realizes the same
interface-level principle in a discrete setting, where compression takes
the form of deciding which components of recursive history survive a
capacity-limited context interface so that they remain useful for later
improvement.

### E.5 Training and Compute Configuration

Table [E.5](#A5.SS5 "E.5 Training and Compute Configuration ‣ Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
summarizes the run-level configurations used for the formal experiments.
Method-specific statistical and feasibility hyperparameters are reported
separately in
Appendix [E.8](#A5.SS8 "E.8 Method Hyperparameters and Sensitivity ‣ Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
The table records the actual experimental configuration rather than host
repository defaults.

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  Configuration |  Temporal Graph |  Language Memory |  VLA |  SRI |
|  Host |  TGN |  CCM-merge |  MemoryVLA |  Meta^(n) |
|  Formal repetitions |  5 random seeds |  fixed CCM-merge host + 3 Ours seeds |  3 independently trained runs |  1 paired search run / backbone |
|  Optimization |  Adam |      AdamW; base LM frozen;  task gradient on CCM comp. + $`\Gamma`$;  predictive gradient on $`\Gamma`$ |  AdamW, frozen host + $`\Gamma`$ |  API-backed recursive search |
|  Batch / accumulation |  200 |  adaptive optimization windows |  $`4\times 4`$ (effective 16) |  – |
|  Primary learning rate |  task $`10^{-4}`$; repr. $`3\times 10^{-4}`$ (UCI formal line) |  frozen formal CCM schedule |  task-LR argument $`10^{-5}`$; $`\Gamma`$ LR $`2\times 10^{-5}`$ |  – |
|  Schedule |  validation-selected |  cosine host schedule |  constant; warmup 0 |  fixed search budget |
|  Maximum budget |  setting-specific formal epoch budget |  60 optimization windows |  25k optimizer steps |  fixed recursive-search budget |
|  Checkpoint cadence |  validation evaluated during training |  CCM-M fixed once; Ours validation-only |  every 1k steps; internal dev selection |  frozen strategy after search |
|  Evaluation repeats |  split-level evaluation |  split-level evaluation |  20 held-out T3 rollouts per selected checkpoint |  3 matched held-out evaluations |
|  Compute |  RTX 5090D |  A100 80GB |  A100 80GB |  API-backed |
|  Software |  Python $`\geq 3.9`$; PyTorch 2.8 environment |  Python $`\geq 3.9`$; PyTorch 2.8 environment |  Python 3.10; PyTorch 2.2.0; Transformers 4.40.1; PEFT 0.11.1 |  Python $`\geq 3.11`$ |

Table 17: Training and compute configuration used in the formal
experiments.

For VLA, the 25k budget is an upper bound rather than a requirement to
report the endpoint. Checkpoints are saved every 1k steps and selected
using only the 20-demonstration internal development split described in
Appendix [C.3](#A3.SS3 "C.3 Vision–Language–Action ‣ Appendix C Experimental Protocols and Evaluation Details ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
After selection, the checkpoint is frozen and evaluated on a separate
set of 20 official held-out T3 evaluation initializations. The reported
SR/SGC rollouts are used for neither training nor checkpoint selection.

### E.6 Efficiency and Model Overhead

We measure computational overhead on the TGN host, where the training
and inference paths can be timed under matched structural settings. All
timings in
Tables [18](#A5.T18 "Table 18 ‣ E.6 Efficiency and Model Overhead ‣ Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
and [19](#A5.T19 "Table 19 ‣ E.6 Efficiency and Model Overhead ‣ Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
use the same RTX 5090D environment within each comparison. The
predictive-feasibility machinery is active only during training;
inference uses the ordinary host recursion with the learned compressor
and performs no feasibility projection.

|         |         |         |               |
|---------|---------|---------|---------------|
| Setting | Vanilla | Ours    | Ratio         |
| 3L10N   | 87.1 s  | 165.7 s | 1.9$`\times`$ |
| 4L5N    | 88.6 s  | 190.6 s | 2.2$`\times`$ |

Table 18: Training-time overhead on TGN. Mean epoch time under matched
settings.

|         |              |           |               |            |
|---------|--------------|-----------|---------------|------------|
| Setting | Vanilla eval | Ours eval | Vanilla build | Ours build |
| 3L10N   | 68.1 s       | 66.0 s    | 1.6 s         | 0.6 s      |
| 4L5N    | 86.7 s       | 87.6 s    | 0.5 s         | 0.7 s      |

Table 19: Inference-time comparison on TGN. The projection is disabled
at inference.

The extra computation is therefore concentrated in training: epoch time
is $`1.9`$–$`2.2\times`$ the vanilla host in these settings. Test-time
evaluation stays within about $`\pm 3\%`$ of vanilla; inference uses no
projection or replay.

##### Parameter overhead.

The vanilla TGN host contains 3,774,385 unique parameters (3.77M). The
compressor adds 381,788 parameters (0.38M), giving approximately 4.16M
unique parameters in total, or a 10.1% increase relative to the host.
The serialized checkpoint contains duplicated references to the
compressor and therefore has a larger stored parameter count; this
duplication does not increase the unique model parameterization used at
runtime.

### E.7 Ablation Study

We further examine which components of the proposed training procedure
contribute to predictive preservation. All ablations are conducted on
UCI link prediction at propagation depth 3 using seed 0. Each
configuration is run once under the same data split, host architecture,
training budget, and evaluation protocol. Accordingly, this experiment
is intended as a controlled mechanism-level diagnosis of the full method
rather than an estimate of cross-seed variability. The corresponding
multi-seed results for the main comparison are reported separately.

|                |                                          |       |       |
|----------------|------------------------------------------|-------|-------|
|  Component     |  Ablation variant                        | AP    | AUC   |
|  Objective     |  Task-Only Training                      | 92.62 | 92.13 |
|                |  History-reconstruction objective        | 90.89 | 90.28 |
|                |  Direct Future Prediction                | 92.76 | 92.24 |
|  Supervision   |  Single-Event Predictive Criterion       | 92.41 | 91.93 |
|                |  Cross-level mispaired supervision       | 92.73 | 92.28 |
|                |  Predictive supervision without context  | 92.89 | 92.50 |
|  Statistics    |  Tree-specific statistical estimation    | 91.48 | 90.99 |
|                |  Unbalanced predictive-test aggregation  | 91.30 | 90.98 |
|                |  Diagonal-covariance approximation       | 91.51 | 91.04 |
|  Optimization  |  Additive Predictive-Gradient Update     | 92.64 | 92.18 |
|                |  Global Predictive Constraint            | 90.32 | 89.88 |
|  Full method   |  Our Method                              | 94.52 | 94.25 |

Table 20: Ablation study on UCI at depth 3 (seed 0). Each variant
changes one component of the full training procedure while keeping the
remaining experimental protocol fixed.

##### Does the choice of preservation objective matter?

Yes. Task-Only Training reaches 92.62 AP and 92.13 AUC, providing the
reference performance when no explicit predictive-preservation objective
is introduced. Replacing predictive preservation with history
reconstruction reduces performance to 90.89 AP and 90.28 AUC, indicating
that retaining information sufficient to reconstruct the past does not
necessarily retain the information useful for subsequent prediction.
Direct Future Prediction performs better at 92.76 AP and 92.24 AUC, but
still remains below the full method at 94.52 AP and 94.25 AUC. This
suggests that predictive targets are more suitable than reconstruction
targets, while directly fitting future outcomes does not recover the
full predictive criterion’s benefit.

##### Does the structure of predictive supervision matter?

The results indicate that it does. The Single-Event Predictive Criterion
reaches 92.41 AP and 91.93 AUC. Replacing the adjacent predictive pair
$`S_{v}=(Y_{v},Y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)})`$ with a single predictive
event weakens the local supervision supplied at each recursive
interface. Cross-level mispaired supervision reaches 92.73 AP and 92.28
AUC. Its degradation relative to the full method indicates that the
correspondence between a compressed state and its associated predictive
event is important, even when comparable predictive events are available
elsewhere in the recursion. Removing the retained context gives 92.89 AP
and 92.50 AUC. The remaining gap to the full method is consistent with
the role of context in defining the conditional predictive information
that should survive each recursive interface.

##### Does the statistical realization of the predictive criterion matter?

All three statistical simplifications lead to a larger reduction.
Estimating the predictive statistics separately for each tree obtains
91.48 AP and 90.99 AUC, suggesting that the shared empirical estimate
benefits from pooling aligned observations across recursive instances.
Removing balancing gives 91.30 AP and 90.98 AUC, supporting the use of
balanced predictive tests when aggregating discrepancies across outcomes
or interfaces. Using a diagonal-covariance approximation gives 91.51 AP
and 91.04 AUC, indicating that cross-dimensional dependence in the
empirical statistic contains useful information. Together, these results
show that implementing the predictive criterion matters beyond the
supervision target.

##### Does constrained optimization contribute beyond the predictive objective?

Yes. The Additive Predictive-Gradient Update yields 92.64 AP and 92.18
AUC, below the 94.52 AP and 94.25 AUC of the full method. This separates
the predictive objective from its predictive-feasibility realization:
using the same supervision as an additive gradient does not provide the
same protection against task proposals that violate predictive
preservation. Replacing the interface-wise constraints with the Global
Predictive Constraint causes the largest drop, to 90.32 AP and 89.88
AUC. The result is consistent with the recursive structure of the
problem: predictive loss can arise at individual recursive interfaces,
and an aggregate global restriction does not provide the same local
control over these violations.

##### Overall.

Across the four groups, the full method achieves the highest AP and AUC
in this fixed seed-0 run. The ablations jointly support four aspects of
the design: predictive rather than reconstructive preservation,
correctly aligned interface-level supervision, the balanced empirical
statistical realization, and interface-wise constrained optimization.
Since the experiment contains one run per configuration, these
comparisons are used to diagnose the internal mechanism of the method;
claims about cross-run variability are based on the separate multi-seed
evaluation.

### E.8 Method Hyperparameters and Sensitivity

##### Method hyperparameters.

summarizes the principal hyperparameters introduced by the
predictive-preservation procedure across the four experimental domains.
We report the frozen configurations used for the formal experiments
rather than command-line defaults. Parameters that belong only to the
underlying host models follow the corresponding host protocols and are
omitted here.

|  |  |  |  |  |

Table 21: Method-specific hyperparameters used in the formal
experiments. Only parameters introduced by the predictive-preservation
procedure or its statistical realization are reported.

##### Hyperparameter selection.

The fixed measurement dimensions, statistical regularization, support
thresholds, and compression capacities are selected using only the
corresponding development or validation data and are frozen before
formal evaluation. No method-specific hyperparameter is selected using
the reported test results. The formal TGN and VLA runs use task-only
optimizer proposals before predictive feasibility projection and
therefore do not use a predictive coefficient $`\lambda`$; the UCI
Additive Predictive-Gradient Update ablation uses $`\lambda=0.00668`$.
The language-memory aggregate specialization uses $`\lambda=0.00594`$.
The feasibility tolerance $`\kappa`$ directly controls the admissible
predictive degradation of an update and is therefore treated as the
principal geometric hyperparameter. We additionally examine the amount
of statistical support through the eligible-interface fraction.

##### Sensitivity analysis.

Figure [9](#A5.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ Sensitivity analysis. ‣ E.8 Method Hyperparameters and Sensitivity ‣ Appendix E Implementation, Ablations, and Reproducibility ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
reports sensitivity on UCI as a representative projection-based setting
in which both the feasibility mechanism and empirical predictive
statistic are active. We vary

|     |                                        |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \kappa\in\{0.01,0.02,0.05,0.10,0.20\},
       ```                                     |     |

with all remaining settings fixed, and separately vary the fraction of
eligible recursive interfaces used to estimate the empirical predictive
statistic. Performance remains stable around the formal operating
region, while substantially tighter or looser feasibility and reduced
statistical support lead to lower performance.

(a) Feasibility tolerance $`\kappa`$

(b) Eligible-interface fraction

## Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs

This appendix collects the formal results supporting the main text. Each
subsection is linked to the corresponding proposition, criterion, or
guarantee used in
Sections [2](#S2 "2 Preliminaries and Problem Formulation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
and [3](#S3 "3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

### F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure

This subsection proves
Proposition [1](#Thmprop1 "Proposition 1 (Recursive Predictive Closure). ‣ 2.2 Predictive Preservation under Recursive Structure ‣ 2 Preliminaries and Problem Formulation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
from
Section [2](#S2 "2 Preliminaries and Problem Formulation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
and states its structural assumptions (M1)–(M3).

This appendix proves
Proposition [1](#Thmprop1 "Proposition 1 (Recursive Predictive Closure). ‣ 2.2 Predictive Preservation under Recursive Structure ‣ 2 Preliminaries and Problem Formulation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
The proof is formulated for the *predictive-interface tree*: every
non-root vertex appearing below is associated with a predictive boundary
variable $`Y_{v}`$. Additional deterministic host operations between
designated predictive interfaces may be absorbed into the corresponding
edge transition. All population statements condition on the full
retained context $`C=(C_{v})_{v\in V}`$; replacing $`C`$ by a local
context $`C_{v}`$ is a separate localization step and is not used in
Proposition [1](#Thmprop1 "Proposition 1 (Recursive Predictive Closure). ‣ 2.2 Predictive Preservation under Recursive Structure ‣ 2 Preliminaries and Problem Formulation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

Let $`\mathcal{T}=(V,E)`$ be a finite rooted tree with root $`\rho`$.
Let $`\operatorname{pa}(v)`$ denote the parent of each non-root node
$`v`$, and let $`\operatorname{ch}(v)`$ denote its children. At
interface $`v`$, let $`Y_{v}`$ denote the predictive boundary state and
let $`U_{v}`$ denote the complete host pre-compression state. Let
$`V_{\mathrm{comp}}\subseteq V\setminus\{\rho\}`$ be the set of
compressed interfaces. For a fixed measurable compression map
$`\Gamma`$, define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
Z_{v}^{\Gamma}:=\begin{cases}\Gamma(U_{v}),&v\in V_{\mathrm{comp}},\\
U_{v},&v\notin V_{\mathrm{comp}},\end{cases}
``` |  |

so that uncompressed nodes are represented by the identity map. Write

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
U:=(U_{v})_{v\neq\rho},\qquad Z^{\Gamma}:=(Z_{v}^{\Gamma})_{v\neq\rho},\qquad C:=(C_{v})_{v\in V}.
``` |  |

When product state spaces are needed below, write

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathsf{U}:=\prod_{v\neq\rho}\mathsf{U}_{v},\qquad\mathsf{Z}:=\prod_{v\neq\rho}\mathsf{Z}_{v}.
``` |  |

All random variables take values in standard Borel spaces.

For every non-root node $`v`$, define the adjacent predictive pair

|     |                                          |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       S_{v}:=(Y_{v},Y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)}),
       ```                                       |     |

and define the tuple of incoming compressed child states

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
R_{v}^{\Gamma}:=Z_{\operatorname{ch}(v)}^{\Gamma}:=\bigl(Z_{u}^{\Gamma}\bigr)_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}.
``` |  |

For a leaf, $`R_{v}^{\Gamma}`$ is the unique element of the singleton
empty-product space. Within the proof we abbreviate
$`Z_{v}:=Z_{v}^{\Gamma}`$ and $`R_{v}:=R_{v}^{\Gamma}`$.

For each compressed node $`v\in V_{\mathrm{comp}}`$, define the local
predictive deficiency

|     |                                                          |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \delta_{v}(\Gamma):=I(S_{v};U_{v}\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C).
       ```                                                       |     |

##### Tree-local recursive Markov structure.

Propositions [1](#Thmprop1 "Proposition 1 (Recursive Predictive Closure). ‣ 2.2 Predictive Preservation under Recursive Structure ‣ 2 Preliminaries and Problem Formulation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
and
[3](#Thmprop3 "Proposition 3 (Root Predictive Stability). ‣ 3.4 Properties and Predictive Guarantees ‣ 3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
use the following structural assumptions for the recursive law induced
by the fixed map $`\Gamma`$.

*(M1) Predictive-boundary Markov tree.* For $`P_{C}`$-almost every
$`c`$, the predictive boundary variables factorize as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
P(dy_{\rho},dy_{-\rho}\mid c)=\Pi_{\rho}(dy_{\rho}\mid c)\prod_{v\neq\rho}Q_{v}(dy_{v}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c).
``` |  | (1) |

*(M2) Recursive host aggregation through compressed child states.* For
every non-root node $`v`$, there is a regular conditional kernel

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
K_{v}(du_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v},c)=P(du_{v}\mid S_{v}=s_{v},R_{v}=r_{v},C=c),
``` |  |

and the conditional law of $`(U_{v},Z_{v})`$ given $`(S_{v},R_{v},C)`$
is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\widetilde{K}_{v}(du_{v},dz_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v},c)=K_{v}(du_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v},c)\,\delta_{\gamma_{v}(u_{v})}(dz_{v}),
``` |  | (2) |

where $`\gamma_{v}=\Gamma`$ on $`V_{\mathrm{comp}}`$ and
$`\gamma_{v}=\operatorname{id}`$ otherwise. Conditioned on
$`(Y_{v},C)`$, the child subtrees rooted at distinct children of $`v`$
are mutually independent. Moreover, conditional on $`(S_{v},R_{v},C)`$,
the local pair $`(U_{v},Z_{v})`$ is independent of all remaining
variables inside the child subtrees; equivalently, the child subtrees
can affect the host state at $`v`$ only through their root compressed
tuple $`R_{v}`$. After $`R_{v}`$ is realized, $`(U_{v},Z_{v})`$ is
formed according to
Equation [2](#A6.E2 "In Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
At the root, its child subtrees are mutually independent conditioned on
$`(Y_{\rho},C)`$. More precisely, conditional on $`C=c`$, the joint law
is the finite child-before-parent composition of $`\Pi_{\rho}`$, all
$`Q_{v}`$, and all $`\widetilde{K}_{v}`$, with $`r_{v}`$ equal to the
sampled child-root $`z`$ tuple. This factorization permits

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
Z_{\operatorname{ch}(v)}^{\Gamma}\longrightarrow U_{v}\longrightarrow Z_{v}^{\Gamma}.
``` |  |

In particular, no independence between $`U_{v}`$ and its incoming
compressed child states is assumed. A deterministic host update of the
form $`U_{v}=F_{v}(R_{v},X_{v},C)`$ is included as the degenerate-kernel
special case, provided the local host input $`X_{v}`$ is part of the
state-generation mechanism represented by $`K_{v}`$.

*(M3) Complete-interface screening.* For every compressed node $`v`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
I\!\left(S_{v};R_{v}^{\Gamma}\,\middle|\,U_{v},C\right)=0,\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad S_{v}\perp R_{v}^{\Gamma}\mid(U_{v},C).
``` |  | (3) |

This is the screening-off property of a complete pre-compression
interface state: once $`U_{v}`$ and the retained context are known, the
incoming compressed child states carry no additional information about
the adjacent predictive interface $`S_{v}`$.
Equation [3](#A6.E3 "In Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
allows $`U_{v}`$ to depend on $`R_{v}^{\Gamma}`$, including through a
deterministic or stochastic aggregation with local host information,
provided the resulting interface law satisfies the stated screening
condition. This condition is additional to (M2). If $`R_{v}^{\Gamma}`$
is a measurable component of $`(U_{v},C)`$, as when the complete
interface record explicitly retains its incoming child tuple, (M3) holds
automatically.

###### Proof of Proposition [1](#Thmprop1 "Proposition 1 (Recursive Predictive Closure). ‣ 2.2 Predictive Preservation under Recursive Structure ‣ 2 Preliminaries and Problem Formulation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

All regular conditional distributions used below exist because the state
spaces are standard Borel. All conditional-kernel identities are
understood outside the corresponding conditioning null sets. Since the
tree is finite, the finitely many almost-sure identities needed in an
induction step can be chosen simultaneously on a common full-measure
set. The recursive kernels below are versions of the conditional laws
under the same induced joint distribution, so substituting these
almost-sure identities into the parent kernel preserves the measure.

Step 1: Zero local predictive deficiency and interface screening imply
augmented local closure.

Fix $`v\in V_{\mathrm{comp}}`$ and abbreviate
$`S_{v}=(Y_{v},Y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)})`$, $`Z_{v}=Z_{v}^{\Gamma}`$,
and $`R_{v}=R_{v}^{\Gamma}`$. By the hypothesis of
Proposition [1](#Thmprop1 "Proposition 1 (Recursive Predictive Closure). ‣ 2.2 Predictive Preservation under Recursive Structure ‣ 2 Preliminaries and Problem Formulation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),

|     |                               |     |     |
|-----|-------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       I(S_{v};U_{v}\mid Z_{v},C)=0.
       ```                            |     | (4) |

By complete-interface screening,

|     |                               |     |     |
|-----|-------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       I(S_{v};R_{v}\mid U_{v},C)=0.
       ```                            |     | (5) |

Because $`Z_{v}=\Gamma(U_{v})`$ is a measurable function of $`U_{v}`$ at
a compressed node,

|     |                                        |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \sigma(U_{v},Z_{v},C)=\sigma(U_{v},C).
       ```                                     |     |

Therefore
Equation [5](#A6.E5 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
is unchanged if $`Z_{v}`$ is added to the conditioning set:

|     |                                     |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       I(S_{v};R_{v}\mid U_{v},Z_{v},C)=0.
       ```                                  |     |

The chain rule for conditional mutual information now gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle I(S_{v};U_{v},R_{v}\mid Z_{v},C)`$ | $`\displaystyle=I(S_{v};U_{v}\mid Z_{v},C)+I(S_{v};R_{v}\mid U_{v},Z_{v},C)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=0.`$ |  |

Hence

|     |                                       |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       S_{v}\perp(U_{v},R_{v})\mid(Z_{v},C).
       ```                                    |     |

In particular, by decomposition of conditional independence,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle U_{v}`$ | $`\displaystyle\perp S_{v}\mid(Z_{v},R_{v},C),`$ |  | (6) |
|  | $`\displaystyle R_{v}`$ | $`\displaystyle\perp S_{v}\mid(Z_{v},C).`$ |  | (7) |

Equation [6](#A6.E6 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
is the form needed in the recursive kernel argument: the local
sufficiency condition remains valid even after the incoming compressed
child tuple is exposed. Notice that this conclusion is derived from the
original defect and the structural screening condition; no additional
“interaction” or node-coupling defect is assumed.

For completeness,
Equation [4](#A6.E4 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
itself is equivalent to conditional independence because

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle I(S_{v};U_{v}\mid Z_{v},C)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{Z_{v},C}\!\left[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left(P_{S_{v},U_{v}\mid Z_{v},C}\,\middle\|\,P_{S_{v}\mid Z_{v},C}\otimes P_{U_{v}\mid Z_{v},C}\right)\right],`$ |  |

and a non-negative Kullback–Leibler divergence has expectation zero only
when it vanishes almost surely.

Step 2: Augmented local closure gives a boundary-independent
reconstruction kernel.

Fix first $`v\in V_{\mathrm{comp}}`$. Under standard Borel regularity, a
regular conditional distribution of $`U_{v}`$ given $`(Z_{v},R_{v},C)`$
exists. Define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
A_{v}(B\mid z_{v},r_{v},c):=P(U_{v}\in B\mid Z_{v}=z_{v},R_{v}=r_{v},C=c),\qquad B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{U}_{v}).
``` |  |

Let $`K_{v}^{\Gamma}`$ be the pushforward of the local host kernel
through $`\Gamma`$:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
K_{v}^{\Gamma}(D\mid s_{v},r_{v},c):=K_{v}\!\left(\Gamma^{-1}(D)\mid s_{v},r_{v},c\right),\qquad D\in\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{Z}_{v}).
``` |  |

Thus $`K_{v}^{\Gamma}(\cdot\mid s_{v},r_{v},c)`$ is the conditional law
of $`Z_{v}`$ given $`(S_{v},R_{v},C)=(s_{v},r_{v},c)`$.

Fix measurable sets $`B\subseteq\mathsf{U}_{v}`$ and
$`D\subseteq\mathsf{Z}_{v}`$. Disintegrating the local joint kernel in
Equation [2](#A6.E2 "In Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
with respect to $`Z_{v}`$ yields

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{v}(B\times D\mid s_{v},r_{v},c)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\int_{D}P(U_{v}\in B\mid S_{v}=s_{v},Z_{v}=z_{v},R_{v}=r_{v},C=c)\,K_{v}^{\Gamma}(dz_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v},c).`$ |  |

By
Equation [6](#A6.E6 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
P(U_{v}\in B\mid S_{v}=s_{v},Z_{v}=z_{v},R_{v}=r_{v},C=c)=A_{v}(B\mid z_{v},r_{v},c)
``` |  |

for $`P_{S_{v},Z_{v},R_{v},C}`$-almost every $`(s_{v},z_{v},r_{v},c)`$.
Therefore, for $`P_{S_{v},R_{v},C}`$-almost every $`(s_{v},r_{v},c)`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\widetilde{K}_{v}(B\times D\mid s_{v},r_{v},c)=\int_{D}A_{v}(B\mid z_{v},r_{v},c)\,K_{v}^{\Gamma}(dz_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v},c).
``` |  |

Since standard Borel $`\sigma`$-algebras are countably generated, the
identity may be chosen on a common full-measure set for a generating
class of rectangles and extended by the monotone class theorem. Hence

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\boxed{\widetilde{K}_{v}(du_{v},dz_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v},c)=A_{v}(du_{v}\mid z_{v},r_{v},c)\,K_{v}^{\Gamma}(dz_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v},c)}\qquad P_{S_{v},R_{v},C}\text{-a.s.}
``` |  | (8) |

The reconstruction kernel may depend on the incoming compressed child
tuple $`r_{v}`$, as it should in a genuine recursive host, but it is
independent of the adjacent predictive pair $`s_{v}`$ once
$`(z_{v},r_{v},c)`$ are fixed.

For an uncompressed non-root node, $`Z_{v}=U_{v}`$. Define

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle K_{v}^{\Gamma}(dz_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v},c)`$ | $`\displaystyle:=K_{v}(dz_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v},c),`$ |  | (9) |
|  | $`\displaystyle A_{v}(du_{v}\mid z_{v},r_{v},c)`$ | $`\displaystyle:=\delta_{z_{v}}(du_{v}).`$ |  | (10) |

Then
Equation [8](#A6.E8 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
holds identically, without requiring either a local predictive
deficiency condition or
Equation [3](#A6.E3 "In Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
Consequently,
Equation [8](#A6.E8 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
is available for every non-root node.

Step 3: Local closure composes recursively over a subtree.

For a non-root node $`v`$, let $`\mathcal{T}_{v}`$ denote the subtree
containing $`v`$ and all its descendants, and write

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
U_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}:=(U_{r})_{r\in V(\mathcal{T}_{v})},\qquad Z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}:=(Z_{r})_{r\in V(\mathcal{T}_{v})}.
``` |  |

Let $`\mathsf{M}_{v}^{U,\Gamma}`$ denote the conditional joint kernel of
$`(U_{\mathcal{T}_{v}},Z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}})`$ given
$`(Y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},C)`$, and let $`\mathsf{M}_{v}^{\Gamma}`$
denote its $`Z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}`$-marginal.

The recursive Markov structure in (M1)–(M2) gives the following precise
child-before-parent construction. For every bounded non-negative
measurable function $`f`$ on
$`\mathsf{U}_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\times\mathsf{Z}_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\int f(u_{\mathcal{T}_{v}},z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}})\,\mathsf{M}_{v}^{U,\Gamma}(du_{\mathcal{T}_{v}},dz_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)`$ |  | (11) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\int Q_{v}(dy_{v}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)\int\bigotimes_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}\mathsf{M}_{u}^{U,\Gamma}(du_{\mathcal{T}_{u}},dz_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}\mid y_{v},c)`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\times\int\widetilde{K}_{v}(du_{v},dz_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v},c)\,f\!\left(u_{v},(u_{\mathcal{T}_{u}})_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)},z_{v},(z_{\mathcal{T}_{u}})_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}\right),`$ |  |  |

where $`s_{v}=(y_{v},y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)})`$ and
$`r_{v}=(z_{u})_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}`$ is read from the root
compressed state of each child subtree. At a leaf, the product kernel
over children is the point mass on the empty tuple.
Equation [11](#A6.E11 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
is a finite composition of probability kernels and therefore defines a
probability kernel by the usual kernel-composition theorem.

Marginalizing the same construction over all $`U`$-variables gives

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{v}^{\Gamma}(dz_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)`$ |  | (12) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\int Q_{v}(dy_{v}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)\left[\bigotimes_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}\mathsf{M}_{u}^{\Gamma}(dz_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}\mid y_{v},c)\right]K_{v}^{\Gamma}(dz_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v},c),`$ |  |  |

where the right-hand side is understood as the same child-before-parent
kernel composition as in
Equation [11](#A6.E11 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle");
in particular $`r_{v}`$ is evaluated from the sampled child-root
$`z`$-coordinates before $`K_{v}^{\Gamma}`$ is applied.

Define reconstruction kernels recursively. At a leaf,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
A_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}(du_{v}\mid z_{v},c):=A_{v}(du_{v}\mid z_{v},r_{v}^{\emptyset},c),
``` |  |

where $`r_{v}^{\emptyset}`$ is the empty tuple. At an internal node,
define

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle A_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}(du_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}},c)`$ |  | (13) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle:=A_{v}(du_{v}\mid z_{v},z_{\operatorname{ch}(v)},c)\bigotimes_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}A_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}(du_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}\mid z_{\mathcal{T}_{u}},c).`$ |  |  |

Because $`z_{\operatorname{ch}(v)}`$ is a measurable coordinate
projection of $`z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}`$ and the tree is finite, this is a
well-defined probability kernel from $`(z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}},c)`$ to
$`u_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}`$.

We prove by induction on the height of $`\mathcal{T}_{v}`$ that, for
every non-root node $`v`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{v}^{U,\Gamma}(du_{\mathcal{T}_{v}},dz_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)`$ |  | (14) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=A_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}(du_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}},c)\,\mathsf{M}_{v}^{\Gamma}(dz_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)`$ |  |  |

for $`P_{Y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},C}`$-almost every
$`(y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)`$.

Equation [14](#A6.E14 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
is the subtree closure statement. It implies that a version of the
conditional law satisfies

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
P(du_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid Z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}=z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}},Y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)}=y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},C=c)=A_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}(du_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}},c),
``` |  |

so reconstruction of the complete pre-compression subtree state depends
on the compressed subtree and retained context but not on the external
boundary state.

*Base case.* Suppose $`v`$ is a leaf. Then $`r_{v}`$ is the empty tuple
and
Equation [11](#A6.E11 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),
applied to a measurable rectangle $`B_{v}\times D_{v}`$, gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{v}^{U,\Gamma}(B_{v}\times D_{v}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\int Q_{v}(dy_{v}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)\,\widetilde{K}_{v}(B_{v}\times D_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v}^{\emptyset},c).`$ |  |

Substituting
Equation [8](#A6.E8 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{v}^{U,\Gamma}(B_{v}\times D_{v}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\int Q_{v}(dy_{v}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)\int_{D_{v}}A_{v}(B_{v}\mid z_{v},r_{v}^{\emptyset},c)\,K_{v}^{\Gamma}(dz_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v}^{\emptyset},c).`$ |  |

The reconstruction factor is independent of both boundary variables once
$`(z_{v},c)`$ are fixed. Tonelli’s theorem therefore gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{v}^{U,\Gamma}(B_{v}\times D_{v}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\int_{D_{v}}A_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}(B_{v}\mid z_{v},c)\,\mathsf{M}_{v}^{\Gamma}(dz_{v}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c),`$ |  |

where the second factor is exactly the leaf case of
Equation [12](#A6.E12 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
Equality on rectangles extends to the full product $`\sigma`$-algebra by
the monotone class theorem, proving
Equation [14](#A6.E14 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
at every leaf.

*Induction step.* Let $`v`$ be an internal node and assume
Equation [14](#A6.E14 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
holds for every $`u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)`$. Fix measurable rectangles

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
B_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}:=B_{v}\times\prod_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}B_{u},\qquad D_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}:=D_{v}\times\prod_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}D_{u},
``` |  |

with $`B_{v}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{U}_{v})`$,
$`D_{v}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{Z}_{v})`$,
$`B_{u}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{U}_{\mathcal{T}_{u}})`$, and
$`D_{u}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{Z}_{\mathcal{T}_{u}})`$. Applying
Equation [11](#A6.E11 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
and then the induction hypothesis to each child gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{v}^{U,\Gamma}(B_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\times D_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\int Q_{v}(dy_{v}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)\int_{\prod_{u}D_{u}}\left[\prod_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}A_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}(B_{u}\mid z_{\mathcal{T}_{u}},c)\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\times\widetilde{K}_{v}(B_{v}\times D_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v},c)\prod_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}\mathsf{M}_{u}^{\Gamma}(dz_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}\mid y_{v},c).`$ |  |

Here and below $`r_{v}=(z_{u})_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}`$ is the tuple
of child root coordinates extracted from the sampled
$`z_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}`$’s. Substitute the local factorization
Equation [8](#A6.E8 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"):

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{v}^{U,\Gamma}(B_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\times D_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\int Q_{v}(dy_{v}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)\int_{\prod_{u}D_{u}}\int_{D_{v}}\left[A_{v}(B_{v}\mid z_{v},r_{v},c)\prod_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}A_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}(B_{u}\mid z_{\mathcal{T}_{u}},c)\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\times K_{v}^{\Gamma}(dz_{v}\mid s_{v},r_{v},c)\prod_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(v)}\mathsf{M}_{u}^{\Gamma}(dz_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}\mid y_{v},c).`$ |  |

All factors are non-negative, so Tonelli’s theorem applies. The
bracketed reconstruction factor depends on $`(z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}},c)`$
only: in particular, although the parent reconstruction is allowed to
depend on the incoming child states $`r_{v}`$, it does not depend on
either $`y_{v}`$ or $`y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)}`$. By
Equation [13](#A6.E13 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),
the bracket is exactly

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
A_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}(B_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}},c).
``` |  |

The remaining $`y_{v}`$- and $`z`$-kernel composition is exactly
$`\mathsf{M}_{v}^{\Gamma}(dz_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)`$
by
Equation [12](#A6.E12 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
Hence

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{v}^{U,\Gamma}(B_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\times D_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\int_{D_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}}A_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}(B_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid z_{\mathcal{T}_{v}},c)\,\mathsf{M}_{v}^{\Gamma}(dz_{\mathcal{T}_{v}}\mid y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)},c).`$ |  |

Equality on measurable rectangles again extends to the full product
$`\sigma`$-algebra by the monotone class theorem. This completes the
induction and proves
Equation [14](#A6.E14 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
for every non-root subtree.

Step 4: Subtree closure gives global conditional independence at the
root.

By (M2), the child subtrees of the root are conditionally independent
given $`(Y_{\rho},C)`$. Since they form a disjoint partition of all
non-root nodes,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
U=(U_{\mathcal{T}_{u}})_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(\rho)},\qquad Z^{\Gamma}=(Z_{\mathcal{T}_{u}})_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(\rho)}.
``` |  |

Therefore, for $`P_{Y_{\rho},C}`$-almost every $`(y_{\rho},c)`$,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
P(du,dz\mid y_{\rho},c)=\bigotimes_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(\rho)}\mathsf{M}_{u}^{U,\Gamma}(du_{\mathcal{T}_{u}},dz_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}\mid y_{\rho},c).
``` |  |

Applying
Equation [14](#A6.E14 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
to each child factor gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle P(du,dz\mid y_{\rho},c)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\left[\bigotimes_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(\rho)}A_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}(du_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}\mid z_{\mathcal{T}_{u}},c)\right]`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\times\left[\bigotimes_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(\rho)}\mathsf{M}_{u}^{\Gamma}(dz_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}\mid y_{\rho},c)\right].`$ |  |

Define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle A_{\mathcal{T}}(du\mid z,c)`$ | $`\displaystyle:=\bigotimes_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(\rho)}A_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}(du_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}\mid z_{\mathcal{T}_{u}},c),`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{\rho}^{\Gamma}(dz\mid y_{\rho},c)`$ | $`\displaystyle:=\bigotimes_{u\in\operatorname{ch}(\rho)}\mathsf{M}_{u}^{\Gamma}(dz_{\mathcal{T}_{u}}\mid y_{\rho},c).`$ |  |

Equivalently, because the tree is finite,

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
A_{\mathcal{T}}(du\mid z,c)=\bigotimes_{v\neq\rho}A_{v}\!\left(du_{v}\mid z_{v},z_{\operatorname{ch}(v)},c\right),
``` |  |

where the factors are kernels in the $`u_{v}`$ coordinates with the full
$`z`$ held fixed. Thus

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
P(du,dz\mid y_{\rho},c)=A_{\mathcal{T}}(du\mid z,c)\,\mathsf{M}_{\rho}^{\Gamma}(dz\mid y_{\rho},c)\qquad P_{Y_{\rho},C}\text{-a.s.}
``` |  | (15) |

Equation [15](#A6.E15 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
is a disintegration of the conditional joint law of $`(U,Z^{\Gamma})`$
given $`(Y_{\rho},C)`$. Hence $`A_{\mathcal{T}}`$ is a version of the
conditional law of $`U`$ given $`(Y_{\rho},Z^{\Gamma},C)`$:

|     |                                                    |     |      |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       P(du\mid y_{\rho},z,c)=A_{\mathcal{T}}(du\mid z,c)
       ```                                                 |     | (16) |

for $`P_{Y_{\rho},Z^{\Gamma},C}`$-almost every $`(y_{\rho},z,c)`$.

It remains only to identify the same kernel as the conditional law given
$`(Z^{\Gamma},C)`$. For measurable $`B\subseteq\mathsf{U}`$, the tower
property and
Equation [16](#A6.E16 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
give

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle P(U\in B\mid z,c)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\int P(U\in B\mid y_{\rho},z,c)\,P(dy_{\rho}\mid z,c)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\int A_{\mathcal{T}}(B\mid z,c)\,P(dy_{\rho}\mid z,c)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=A_{\mathcal{T}}(B\mid z,c).`$ |  |

Therefore

|     |                                      |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       P(du\mid y_{\rho},z,c)=P(du\mid z,c)
       ```                                   |     |

for $`P_{Y_{\rho},Z^{\Gamma},C}`$-almost every $`(y_{\rho},z,c)`$, i.e.

|     |                                    |     |      |
|-----|------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       Y_{\rho}\perp U\mid(Z^{\Gamma},C).
       ```                                 |     | (17) |

Step 5: Global conditional independence preserves the root law.

Since $`Z^{\Gamma}`$ is a measurable function of $`U`$,

|     |                                     |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \sigma(U,Z^{\Gamma},C)=\sigma(U,C).
       ```                                  |     |

Let $`D`$ be a measurable subset of the state space of $`Y_{\rho}`$.
Therefore

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
P(Y_{\rho}\in D\mid U,C)=P(Y_{\rho}\in D\mid U,Z^{\Gamma},C)\qquad P\text{-a.s.}
``` |  |

By
Equation [17](#A6.E17 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle"),

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
P(Y_{\rho}\in D\mid U,Z^{\Gamma},C)=P(Y_{\rho}\in D\mid Z^{\Gamma},C)\qquad P\text{-a.s.}
``` |  |

Combining the two equalities and using a countable determining class for
the standard Borel state space of $`Y_{\rho}`$ yields

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}(Y_{\rho}\mid U,C)=\mathcal{L}(Y_{\rho}\mid Z^{\Gamma},C)\qquad P\text{-a.s.}
``` |  |

Finally,
Equation [17](#A6.E17 "In Proof of Proposition . ‣ Tree-local recursive Markov structure. ‣ F.1 Proof of Recursive Closure ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
and the conditional Kullback–Leibler characterization of mutual
information give

|     |                                   |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       I(Y_{\rho};U\mid Z^{\Gamma},C)=0.
       ```                                |     |

This proves the theorem. ∎

### F.2 Local Variational Characterization

This subsection establishes the exact variational identity underlying
the predictive-deficiency construction in
Section [3](#S3 "3 Learning for Predictive Preservation ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").
In particular,
Proposition [4](#Thmprop4 "Proposition 4 (Local variational characterization). ‣ F.2 Local Variational Characterization ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
gives the population criterion from which the empirical surrogate is
derived.

This appendix gives the exact variational form of the local predictive
deficiency used in the main text. For a compressed node
$`v\in V_{\mathrm{comp}}`$, write

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
S_{v}:=(Y_{v},Y_{\operatorname{pa}(v)}),\qquad Z_{v}^{\Gamma}:=\Gamma(U_{v}),
``` |  |

and

|     |                                                          |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \delta_{v}(\Gamma):=I(S_{v};U_{v}\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C).
       ```                                                       |     |

All variables are assumed to take values in standard Borel spaces and
$`\Gamma`$ is measurable, so the regular conditional distributions below
exist. Kullback–Leibler divergences are understood in the extended-real
sense.

For any measurable conditional distribution
$`q_{v}(\cdot\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C)`$ of $`S_{v}`$, define

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{V}_{v}(\Gamma,q_{v}):=\mathbb{E}\!\left[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left(P_{S_{v}\mid U_{v},C}\,\middle\|\,q_{v}(\cdot\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C)\right)\right].
``` |  |

###### Proposition 4 (Local variational characterization).

For every measurable compressor $`\Gamma`$ and every compressed node
$`v`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\boxed{\mathcal{V}_{v}(\Gamma,q_{v})=\delta_{v}(\Gamma)+\mathbb{E}\!\left[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left(P_{S_{v}\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C}\,\middle\|\,q_{v}(\cdot\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C)\right)\right].}
``` |  | (18) |

Consequently,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\boxed{\delta_{v}(\Gamma)=\inf_{q_{v}}\mathcal{V}_{v}(\Gamma,q_{v}),}
``` |  | (19) |

and the infimum is attained by

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
q_{v}^{\star}(\cdot\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C)=P_{S_{v}\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C}.
``` |  |

If the common value is finite, every minimizer agrees with
$`q_{v}^{\star}`$ for $`P_{Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C}`$-almost every conditioning
value.

###### Proof.

Suppress the node index and write

|     |                                                    |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       S:=S_{v},\qquad U:=U_{v},\qquad Z:=Z_{v}^{\Gamma}.
       ```                                                 |     |

Because $`Z=\Gamma(U)`$ is measurable with respect to $`U`$,

|     |                                                   |     |      |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       P_{S\mid U,Z,C}=P_{S\mid U,C}\qquad P\text{-a.s.}
       ```                                                |     | (20) |

For $`P_{Z,C}`$-almost every $`(z,c)`$, the conditional relative-entropy
chain rule gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\int D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left(P_{S\mid U=u,Z=z,C=c}\,\middle\|\,q_{v}(\cdot\mid z,c)\right)P_{U\mid Z=z,C=c}(du)`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=I(S;U\mid Z=z,C=c)+D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left(P_{S\mid Z=z,C=c}\,\middle\|\,q_{v}(\cdot\mid z,c)\right).`$ |  |

Integrating over $`(Z,C)`$ and using
Equation [20](#A6.E20 "In Proof. ‣ F.2 Local Variational Characterization ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
yields
Equation [18](#A6.E18 "In Proposition 4 (Local variational characterization). ‣ F.2 Local Variational Characterization ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle").

The second term in
Equation [18](#A6.E18 "In Proposition 4 (Local variational characterization). ‣ F.2 Local Variational Characterization ‣ Appendix F Theoretical Results and Proofs ‣ What to Preserve in Recursive Computation: A Local Predictive Sufficiency Principle")
is non-negative and vanishes at
$`q_{v}=q_{v}^{\star}=P_{S_{v}\mid Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C}`$. Therefore

|     |                                                               |     |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \inf_{q_{v}}\mathcal{V}_{v}(\Gamma,q_{v})=\delta_{v}(\Gamma).
       ```                                                            |     |

If the optimum is finite, equality requires the remaining conditional
Kullback–Leibler divergence to vanish almost surely, which gives the
stated uniqueness up to $`P_{Z_{v}^{\Gamma},C}`$-null sets. ∎

##### Zero-defect interpretation.

Since $`Z_{v}^{\Gamma}`$ is a measurable function of $`U_{v}`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
````
